## 4.A Derivation of the model
Consider the RBM Hamiltonian

$$
-\beta H\left[\mathbf{x}, \mathbf{q}, \mathbf{h} ; \mathbf{J}\right]=\beta \sum_{\mu=1}^{P} h_{\mu} \sum_{i=1}^{N} w_{i}^{\mu} x_{i}+\beta \sum_{\mu=1}^{P} h_{\mu} \sum_{y=1}^{C} u_{y}^{\mu} q_{y}+\beta \sum_{\mu=1}^{P} h_{\mu} b^{\mu}.
$$

where $\mathbf{J}=\{\mathbf{w}, \mathbf{u}, \mathbf{b}\}$ is the set of all RBM parameters, i.e. $\mathbf{w}=\left\{w_{i}^{\mu}\right\}_{1 \leq i \leq N}^{1 \leq \mu \leq P}$, $\mathbf{u}=\left\{u_{i}^{\mu}\right\}_{1 \leq i \leq C}^{1 \leq \mu \leq P}$ and $\mathbf{b}=\left\{b^{\mu}\right\}_{\mu=1}^{P}$. The prior densities $\mathrm{P}_{0}(\mathbf{h})$ and $\mathrm{P}_{0}(\mathbf{q})$ are nonzero only when $\mathbf{h} \in\left\{\mathbf{e}_{\gamma}\right\}_{\gamma=0}^{P}$ and $\mathbf{q} \in\left\{\mathbf{e}_{y}\right\}_{y=0}^{C}$, in which case we have

$$
-\beta H\left[\mathbf{x}, \mathbf{e}_{y}, \mathbf{e}_{\gamma} ; \mathbf{J}\right]= \begin{cases}0 & \gamma=0 \\ \beta \sum_{i=1}^{N} w_{i}^{\gamma} x_{i}+a_{y}^{\gamma} & 0<\gamma \leq P\end{cases}
$$

$$
\text { where } \quad a_{y}^{\mu}= \begin{cases}\beta b^{\mu} & y=0 \\ \beta u_{y}^{\mu}+\beta b^{\mu} & 0<y \leq C\end{cases}
$$

For convenience, we write the corresponding Gibbs distribution $\mathrm{P}_{\beta}\left(\mathbf{x}, \mathbf{q}=\mathbf{e}_{y}, \mathbf{h}=\mathbf{e}_{\gamma} | \mathbf{J}\right)$ as $\mathrm{P}_{\beta}(\mathbf{x}, y, \mu | \mathbf{J})$, and the priors $\mathrm{P}_{0}\left(\mathbf{q}=\mathbf{e}_{y}\right)$ and $\mathrm{P}_{0}\left(\mathbf{h}=\mathbf{e}_{\gamma}\right)$ as $\pi_{\mathbf{q}}(y)$ and $\pi_{\mathbf{h}}(\gamma)$, respectively. Given a uniform prior on $\mathbf{x}$, we find

$$
\mathrm{P}_{\beta}(\mathbf{x}, y, \mu | \mathbf{J})=\frac{1}{Z_{\beta}(\mathbf{J})} \pi_{\mathbf{q}}(y) \begin{cases}\pi_{\mathbf{h}}(0) & \gamma=0 \\ \pi_{\mathbf{h}}(\gamma) \exp \left(a_{y}^{\gamma}\right) \exp \left(\beta \sum_{i=1}^{N} w_{i}^{\gamma} x_{i}\right) & 0<\gamma \leq P,\end{cases}
$$

where $Z_{\beta}(\mathbf{J})$ is an unknown normalization constant. The data $\mathbf{x}$ belonging to each cluster $\mu>0$ follows a von Mises-Fisher (vMF) distribution $\mathrm{P}_{\beta}(\mathbf{x} | \mu, \mathbf{J}) \propto \exp \left(\beta \sum_{i=1}^{N} w_{i}^{\mu} x_{i}\right)$ centered on $\mathbf{w}^{\mu}=\left\{w_{i}^{\mu}\right\}_{i=1}^{N}$ (see Appendix 4.B). We assume that $\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(w_{i}^{\mu}\right)^{2}}=1$ so that the corresponding cluster centroids $\mathbf{w}^{\mu}$ lie on the

hypersphere $S^{N-1}$ like the data $\mathbf{x} = \{x_i\}_{i=1}^N$. We marginalize $\mathrm{P}_\beta (\mathbf{x}, y, \mu|\mathbf{J})$ over the hidden units $\mu$ and get

$$
\mathrm{P}_\beta (\mathbf{x}, y|\mathbf{J}) = \frac{1}{Z_\beta (\mathbf{J})} \pi_{\mathbf{q}} (y) \left[ \sum_{\mu=1}^P \pi_{\mathbf{h}} (\mu) \exp \left(a_y^\mu\right) \exp \left( \beta \sum_{i=1}^N w_i^\mu x_i \right) + \pi_{\mathbf{h}} (0) \right].
$$

We will now find the normalization constant $Z_\beta (\mathbf{J})$. Marginalizing $\mathrm{P}_\beta (\mathbf{x}, y|\mathbf{J})$ over $\mathbf{x}$, we obtain

$$
\begin{aligned}
\mathrm{P}_\beta (y|\mathbf{J}) &= \int_{S^{N-1}} d\mathbf{x} \, \mathrm{P}_\beta (\mathbf{x}, y|\mathbf{J}) \\
&= \frac{1}{Z_\beta (\mathbf{J})} \pi_{\mathbf{q}} (y) \int_{S^{N-1}} d\mathbf{x} \left[ \sum_{\mu=1}^P \pi_{\mathbf{h}} (\mu) \exp \left(a_y^\mu\right) \exp \left( \beta \sum_{i=1}^N w_i^\mu x_i \right) + \pi_{\mathbf{h}} (0) \right] \\
&= \frac{1}{Z_\beta (\mathbf{J})} \pi_{\mathbf{q}} (y) \, \Omega_N (\beta) \sum_{\mu=1}^P \pi_{\mathbf{h}} (\mu) \exp \left(a_y^\mu\right) + \Omega_N (0) \pi_{\mathbf{h}} (0) \\
&= \frac{1}{Z_\beta (\mathbf{J})} \pi_{\mathbf{q}} (y) \, \Omega_N (\beta) \left[ \sum_{\mu=1}^P \pi_{\mathbf{h}} (\mu) \exp \left(a_y^\mu\right) + \pi_{\mathbf{h}} (0) \exp \left(a^0\right) \right],
\end{aligned}
$$

where $a^0 = \log [\Omega_N (0)/\Omega_N (\beta)]$. Normalization of $\mathrm{P}_\beta (y|\mathbf{J})$ requires that

$$
\mathrm{P}_\beta (y|\mathbf{J}) = \frac{\pi_{\mathbf{q}} (y) \left[ \sum_{\mu=1}^P \pi_{\mathbf{h}} (\mu) \exp \left(a_y^\mu\right) + \pi_{\mathbf{h}} (0) \exp \left(a^0\right) \right]}{\sum_{y'=0}^C \pi_{\mathbf{q}} (y') \left[ \sum_{\nu=1}^P \pi_{\mathbf{h}} (\nu) \exp \left(a_{y'}^\nu\right) + \pi_{\mathbf{h}} (0) \exp \left(a^0\right) \right]},
$$

so we deduce that $Z_\beta (\mathbf{J}) = \Omega_N (\beta) \sum_{y=0}^C \pi_{\mathbf{q}} (y) \left[ \sum_{\mu=1}^P \pi_{\mathbf{h}} (\mu) \exp \left(a_y^\mu\right) + \pi_{\mathbf{h}} (0) \exp \left(a^0\right) \right]$. We define

$$
p_y^\gamma =
\begin{cases}
\frac{\pi_{\mathbf{q}}(y)\pi_{\mathbf{h}}(0)\exp(a^0)}{\sum_{y'=0}^C \pi_{\mathbf{q}} (y') \left[ \sum_{\nu=1}^P \pi_{\mathbf{h}} (\nu) \exp \left(a_{y'}^\nu\right) + \pi_{\mathbf{h}} (0) \exp \left(a^0\right) \right]} & \gamma = 0 \\
\frac{\pi_{\mathbf{q}}(y)\pi_{\mathbf{h}}(\mu)\exp(a_y^\mu)}{\sum_{y'=0}^C \pi_{\mathbf{q}} (y') \left[ \sum_{\nu=1}^P \pi_{\mathbf{h}} (\nu) \exp \left(a_{y'}^\nu\right) + \pi_{\mathbf{h}} (0) \exp \left(a^0\right) \right]} & 0 < \gamma \leq P,
\end{cases}
$$

from which we obtain

$$
\mathrm{P}_\beta (\mathbf{x}, y|\mathbf{J}) = \sum_{\mu=1}^P p_y^\mu \frac{\exp \left( \beta \sum_{i=1}^N w_i^\mu x_i \right)}{\Omega_N (\beta)} + p_y^0 \frac{1}{\Omega_N (0)}.
$$

The conditional distributions $\mathrm{P}_\beta (\mathbf{x}|y, \mathbf{J})$ and their marginal $\mathrm{P}_\beta (\mathbf{x}|\mathbf{J}) = \sum_{y=0}^C \mathrm{P}_\beta (\mathbf{x}|y, \mathbf{J}) \mathrm{P}_\beta (y|\mathbf{J})$ are von Mises-Fisher mixtures [205] with weights $\frac{p_y^\gamma}{\sum_{\nu=0}^P p_y^\nu}$ and $\sum_{y=0}^C p_y^\gamma$, respectively. As explained in Section 4.2, we constrain the marginals $\sum_{y=0}^C p_y^\gamma = \mathrm{P}_\beta (\gamma|\mathbf{J})$ and $\sum_{\gamma=0}^P p_y^\gamma = \mathrm{P}_\beta (y|\mathbf{J})$ to be equal to fixed distributions $p_{\mathbf{h}} (\gamma)$ and $p_{\mathbf{q}} (y)$, respectively. Formally, this means that $\mathbf{p} = \{p_y^\gamma\}_{0 \leq y \leq C}^{0 \leq \gamma \leq C}$ belongs to the transportation polytope with sum constraints $\sum_{\gamma=0}^P p_y^\gamma = p_{\mathbf{q}} (y)$ and $\sum_{y=0}^C p_y^\gamma = p_{\mathbf{h}} (\gamma)$ [218].

121

### 4.B Integration of the von Mises-Fisher density

The von Mises-Fisher (vMF) distribution [219] is an isotropic Gaussian distribution restricted to the $N-1$ dimensional unit sphere $S^{N-1}$. It takes the form

$$
p(\mathbf{x}) = \Omega(r)^{-1} \exp\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N} r_i x_i\right),
$$

where $r = \sqrt{\sum_i [r_i]^2}$ is called the concentration parameter. When $r=0$, it reduces to the uniform distribution on the unit sphere, whose surface surface area $\frac{2\pi^{N/2}}{\Gamma(N/2)}$ is thus also the normalization constant $\Omega_N(0)$. When $r>0$, we define the mean direction $\hat{\mathbf{r}} = \{r_i/r\}_{i=1}^N$ and find the normalization constant to be

$$
\begin{aligned}
\Omega_N(r) &= \int_{S^{N-1}} d\mathbf{x} \exp\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N} r_i x_i\right) \label{eq:4.17} \\
&= \int_{S^{N-1}} d\mathbf{x} \exp\left(r \sum_{i=1}^{N} \hat{r}_i x_i\right) \\
&= \Omega_{N-1}(0) \int_{-1}^{1} du\ (1-u^2)^{(N-3)/2} \exp(ru) \\
&= \Omega_N(0) \left(\frac{r}{2}\right)^{1-N/2} \Gamma\left(\frac{N}{2}\right) I_{N/2-1}(r),
\end{aligned}
$$

where $I_n(x)$ is the modified Bessel function of the first kind of order $n$. The third line of (4.17) comes from the change of variables $u = \sum_{i=1}^{N} \hat{r}_i x_i$, and the fourth line is a consequence of Poisson's Bessel function integral [220]. In the limit of large $N$ with $\rho = \frac{r}{N-2} \approx \frac{r}{N}$, we find

$$
\begin{aligned}
\int_{S^{N-1}} d\mathbf{x} \exp\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N} r_i x_i\right) &\approx \frac{\Omega_N(0)}{\sqrt{2\pi(N/2-1)}} \left(\frac{N}{2}-1\right)^{1-N/2} \left(1+(2\rho)^2\right)^{-1/4} \\
&\quad \Gamma\left(\frac{N}{2}\right) \exp\left[\left(\frac{N}{2}-1\right)(\eta(2\rho)+1)\right]
\end{aligned}
$$

where $\eta(x) = (1+x^2)^{1/2} - 1 - \log\left[1+(1+x^2)^{1/2}\right] + \log 2$,

by exploiting the large $N$ asymptotic expansion of $I_{N/2-1}([N/2-1]\cdot 2\rho)$ found in [221, 222]. We use Stirling's approximation

$$
\Gamma\left(\frac{N}{2}\right) \approx \sqrt{2\pi(N/2-1)} \left(\frac{N}{2}-1\right)^{N/2-1} \exp\left(-\frac{N}{2}+1\right)
$$

to simplify it to

$$
\int_{S^{N-1}} d \mathbf{x} \exp \left(\sum_{i=1}^{N} r_{i} x_{i}\right) \approx \Omega_{N}(0)\left(1+(2 \rho)^{2}\right)^{-1 / 4} \exp \left[\left(\frac{N}{2}-1\right) \eta(2 \rho)\right]
\tag{4.18}
$$

$$
\text{where } \eta(x)=\left(1+x^{2}\right)^{1 / 2}-1-\log \left[1+\left(1+x^{2}\right)^{1 / 2}\right]+\log 2.
$$

## 4.C Normalization of the weights

In order to enforce $\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(w_{i}^{\mu}\right)^{2}}=1$ at each SGD step, we project $\mathbf{w}^{\mu}$ and the gradient of the loss with respect to $\mathbf{w}^{\mu}$ onto the unit sphere $S^{N-1}$ and the tangent space of $\mathbf{w}^{\mu}$, respectively. We divide $w_{i}^{\mu}$ by its norm to project it onto $S^{N-1}$, and we multiply the gradient by $\delta_{j k}-w_{j}^{\mu} w_{k}^{\mu}$ to project it onto the tangent space. Projecting the gradient onto the tangent space is a mathematically sound way to obtain the gradient of a function restricted to a manifold embedded in $\mathbb{R}^{N}$ [223].

The tasks of deriving the saddle-point equations for $\bar{p}_{y}^{\gamma}$ (Eqs. 4.10), finding the stationarity condition of the loss with respect to the class weights $p_{y}^{\gamma}$ (Eqs. 4.6) and efficiently training $p_{y}^{\gamma}$ all amount to solving the extremization problem

$$
\underset{\mathbf{p}, \omega, \lambda}{\operatorname{Extr}}\left\{f(\mathbf{p})+\sum_{y=0}^{C} \lambda_{y}\left(\sum_{\gamma=0}^{P} p_{y}^{\gamma}-p_{\mathbf{q}}(y)\right)+\sum_{\gamma=0}^{P} \omega^{\gamma}\left(\sum_{y=0}^{C} p_{y}^{\gamma}-p_{\mathbf{h}}(\gamma)\right)\right\},
\tag{4.19}
$$

where $\lambda_{y}$ and $\omega^{\gamma}$ are Lagrange multipliers that enforce the constraints $\sum_{\gamma} p_{y}^{\gamma}=p_{\mathbf{q}}(y)$ and $\sum_{y} p_{y}^{\gamma}=p_{\mathbf{h}}(\gamma)$. In the former task, $f(\mathbf{p})$ is the free entropy (Eq. 4.26) at fixed $\mathfrak{m}$ and $\hat{\mathfrak{m}}$. In the latter two, it is the negative log-likelihood loss (Eq. 4.4) at a given $\mathbf{w}$. To find the saddle-point equations and the stationarity conditions of the loss, we derive an implicit solution of Eq. (4.19) that is useful in analytical calculations. On the other hand, to train $p_{y}^{\gamma}$, we design an algorithm to quickly compute a numerical solution of Eq. (4.19). We start by noting that, since the extrema of Eq. (4.19) are the points where the gradient vanishes, they take the form

$$
\begin{aligned}
\partial_{p_{y}^{\gamma}} f(\mathbf{p}) &=\lambda_{y}+\omega^{\gamma} \\
\sum_{\gamma=0}^{P} p_{y}^{\gamma} &=p_{\mathbf{q}}(y) \\
\sum_{y=0}^{C} p_{y}^{\gamma} &=p_{\mathbf{h}}(\gamma).
\end{aligned}
\tag{4.20}
$$

Define $\bar{p}_{y}^{\gamma}=\partial_{p_{y}^{\gamma}} \exp (f(\mathbf{p}))=p_{y}^{\gamma} \partial_{p_{y}^{\gamma}} f(\mathbf{p})$, then

$$
\frac{\bar{p}_{y}^{\gamma}}{p_{y}^{\gamma}}=\lambda_{y}+\omega^{\gamma},
$$

along with the previously established row and column sum constraints on $\mathbf{p}$. Rearranging terms, we get

$$
p_{y}^{\gamma}=\frac{1}{\lambda_{y}+\omega^{\gamma}} \bar{p}_{y}^{\gamma}.
$$

123

Using the row and column constraints $\sum_{\gamma=0}^P p_y^\gamma = p_{\mathbf{q}}(y)$ and $\sum_{y=0}^C p_y^\gamma = p_{\mathbf{h}}(\gamma)$, we find that the Lagrange multipliers $\lambda_y$ and $\omega^\gamma$ solve the nonlinear equations

$$
\begin{aligned}
\lambda_y &= \frac{1}{p_{\mathbf{q}}(y)} \sum_{\gamma=0}^P \frac{\lambda_y}{\lambda_y + \omega^\gamma} \bar{p}_y^\gamma \\
\omega^\gamma &= \frac{1}{p_{\mathbf{h}}(\gamma)} \sum_{y=0}^C \frac{\omega^\gamma}{\lambda_y + \omega^\gamma} \bar{p}_y^\gamma,
\end{aligned}
\tag{4.21}
$$

For conciseness, we define $\zeta_y^\gamma (\overline{\mathbf{p}}; p_{\mathbf{h}}) = \lambda_y (\overline{\mathbf{p}}; p_{\mathbf{h}}) + \omega^\gamma (\overline{\mathbf{p}}; p_{\mathbf{h}})$, where $\lambda_y (\overline{\mathbf{p}}; p_{\mathbf{h}})$ and $\omega^\gamma (\overline{\mathbf{p}}; p_{\mathbf{h}})$ are the $\lambda_y$ and $\omega^\gamma$ solving Eqs. (4.21) at given $P$ and $p_{\mathbf{h}}$. Using these definitions, we find the implicit solution $p_y^\gamma = \bar{p}_y^\gamma / \zeta_y^\gamma (\overline{\mathbf{p}}; p_{\mathbf{h}})$. As wanted, this equation is useful in analytical calculations involving the saddle-point equations and the stationarity conditions of the loss. However, it is also quite slow to solve numerically for a given $\overline{\mathbf{p}}$, as reported in multiple studies [224, 225, 226]. Therefore, although we can train $p_y^\gamma$ by iterating $p_y^\gamma = \bar{p}_y^\gamma / \zeta_y^\gamma (\overline{\mathbf{p}}; p_{\mathbf{h}})$, it is not a very efficient method.

In order to efficiently train $p_y^\gamma$, we devise a faster way to solve Eqs. (4.20) than through Eq. (4.21). Exponentiating both sides of the first line of Eqs. (4.20), we find

$$
\begin{aligned}
\exp \left( \eta \partial_{p_y^\gamma} f (\mathbf{p}) \right) \exp (-\eta \lambda_y) \exp (-\eta \omega^\gamma) &= 1 \\
\exp (-\eta \omega^\gamma) p_y^\gamma \exp \left( \eta \partial_{p_y^\gamma} f (\mathbf{p}) \right) \exp (-\eta \lambda_y) &= p_y^\gamma,
\end{aligned}
$$

where $\eta$ is an arbitrary scalar that will play the role of a learning rate. Fix $k_y^\gamma = p_y^\gamma \exp \left( \eta \partial_{p_y^\gamma} f (\mathbf{p}) \right)$. By the Sinkhorn-Knopp theorem [227, 228], there is a rescaled matrix of the form $p_y'^\gamma = D_L^\gamma (\mathbf{k}) k_y^\gamma D_R^y (\mathbf{k})$ that satisfies the same constraints $p_y'^\gamma \geq 0$, $\sum_{\gamma=0}^P p_y'^\gamma = p_{\mathbf{q}}(y)$ and $\sum_{y=0}^C p_y'^\gamma = p_{\mathbf{h}}(\gamma)$ as $p_y^\gamma$ if some technical conditions are satisfied [229]. Moreover, if $p_y'^\gamma$ exists, then it is unique [230, 231], and we can quickly compute suitable scaling factors $D_L^\gamma (\mathbf{k})$ and $D_R^\gamma (\mathbf{k})$ for $\mathbf{k} = \left\{ k_y^\gamma \right\}_{0 \leq y \leq C}^{0 \leq \gamma \leq P}$ using the Sinkhorn-Knopp algorithm first proposed in [232] and theoretically justified in subsequent works [227, 228]. See [229] for a concise review of all these points. $p_y'^\gamma$ is generally not equal to $p_y^\gamma$. However, we observe that the iteration

$$
\begin{aligned}
k_y^\gamma (t) &= p_y^\gamma (t) \exp \left( \eta \partial_{p_y^\gamma (t)} f (\mathbf{p} (t)) \right) \\
p_y^\gamma (t+1) &= D_L^\gamma (\mathbf{k} (t)) k_y^\gamma (t) D_R^y (\mathbf{k} (t))
\end{aligned}
\tag{4.22}
$$

converges at small $\eta$ (for example $\sim 0.1/P$), which means that it must converge to a solution of Eqs. (4.20). Since the Sinkhorn-Knopp algorithm is fast, it is significantly more efficient to iterate Eqs. (4.22) than $p_y^\gamma = \bar{p}_y^\gamma / \zeta_y^\gamma (\overline{\mathbf{p}}; p_{\mathbf{h}})$ to solve Eqs. (4.20).

In practice, we train $p_y^\gamma$ using a stochastic variant of Eqs. (4.22) where the gradient is estimated over small batches of data and smoothed with a momentum hyperparameter. Furthermore, we multiply the gradient by $\delta_{yy'} - p_y^\gamma / p_{\mathbf{h}} (\gamma)$ to reduce the size of the components of $\exp \left( \eta \partial_{p_y^\gamma (t)} f (\mathbf{p} (t)) \right)$ that move $p_y^\gamma$ away from the constraints $\sum_{\gamma=0}^P p_y^\gamma = p_{\mathbf{q}}(y)$ and $\sum_{y=0}^C p_y^\gamma = p_{\mathbf{h}}(\gamma)$. This step is a simple approximation of the gradient projection proposed in [233].

124

### 4.D Initialization and learning rate

To make all our figures, except Fig. (4.6), we initialize the memories $\mathbf{w}^\mu$ uniformly at random on the unit hypersphere $S^{N-1}$ [234]. To make Fig. 4.6, we instead use the algorithm of [235] to sample the initial memories $\mathbf{w}^\mu$ from a vMF distribution (see Appendix 4.B) with mean direction $\tilde{\mathbf{x}}^* = \bar{\mathbf{x}}^*/\|\bar{\mathbf{x}}^*\|$, where $\bar{\mathbf{x}}^* = \frac{1}{N} \sum_{\mu_*=1}^{P^*} \mathbf{x}^{*\mu_*}$ is the mean of the patterns $\mathbf{x}^{*\mu_*}$ and $\|\bar{\mathbf{x}}^*\| = \sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^N [\bar{x}_i^*]^2}$. By construction, the mean direction $\tilde{\mathbf{x}}^*$ is the ordered solution of Eq. (4.10) with the most permutation symmetries, or in other words the root of the learning dynamics tree shown in Fig. 4.6, so initializing the memories around it helps reveal the tree structure.

We use a learning rate of $\eta \sim 0.1$ to train the memories $\mathbf{w}^\mu$. Based on our experience, $\mathbf{p}$ trains well when its own learning rate is approximately $\left(1 + \frac{p_{\mathbf{h}}(0)}{1-p_{\mathbf{h}}(0)}\right) P$ times smaller. Without this rescaling, the multiplicative update factor $\exp\left(\eta \partial_{p_y^\gamma} L(\mathbf{w}, \mathbf{p})\right)$ described in Appendix 4.C can be relatively large even when $\eta$ is relatively small, making it ill behaved. Equivalently, we can also train $g_y^\gamma = \left(1 + \frac{p_{\mathbf{h}}(0)}{1-p_{\mathbf{h}}(0)}\right) P \mathbf{p}$ with the same learning rate $\eta$ as $\mathbf{w}$. We adopt this approach in our code available at [197].

Let $P_0 = \frac{p_{\mathbf{h}}(0)}{1-p_{\mathbf{h}}(0)} P$ so that $g_y^\gamma = (P + P_0) p_y^\gamma$. In terms of $g_y^{\text{fixed},\gamma} = (P + P_0) p_y^{\text{fixed},\gamma}$, the duplicated parameters (Eq. 4.13) of the saddle-point hierarchy principle are

$$
\bar{x}_i^{\text{dupli},\mu} =
\begin{cases}
\bar{x}_i^{\text{fixed},\mu} & 0 < \mu \leq P \\
\bar{x}_i^{\text{fixed},\mu-P} & P < \mu \leq P + R
\end{cases}
$$

$$
\bar{g}_y^{\text{dupli},\gamma} = \frac{P + R}{P}
\begin{cases}
\bar{g}_y^{\text{fixed},0} & \gamma = 0 \\
\frac{1}{2} \bar{g}_y^{\text{fixed},\gamma} & 0 < \gamma \leq R \\
\bar{g}_y^{\text{fixed},\gamma} & R < \gamma \leq P \\
\frac{1}{2} \bar{g}_y^{\text{fixed},\gamma-P} & P < \gamma \leq P + R
\end{cases}
$$

$$
m^{\text{dupli},\mu_* \gamma} =
\begin{cases}
m^{\text{fixed},\mu_* 0} & \gamma = 0 \\
m^{\text{fixed},\mu_* \gamma} & 0 < \gamma \leq P \\
m^{\text{fixed},\mu_*,\gamma-P} & P < \gamma \leq P + R
\end{cases}
$$

$$
g_y^{\text{dupli},\gamma} = \frac{P + R}{P}
\begin{cases}
g_y^{\text{fixed},0} & \gamma = 0 \\
\frac{1}{2} g_y^{\text{fixed},\gamma} & 0 < \gamma \leq R \\
g_y^{\text{fixed},\gamma} & R < \gamma \leq P \\
\frac{1}{2} g_y^{\text{fixed},\gamma-P} & P < \gamma \leq P + R
\end{cases}
$$

$$
\text{along with } \quad g_{\mathbf{h}}(\gamma) = \frac{P + R}{P}
\begin{cases}
g_{\mathbf{h}}^{\text{given}}(0) & \gamma = 0 \\
\frac{1}{2} g_{\mathbf{h}}^{\text{given}}(\gamma) & 0 < \gamma \leq R \\
g_{\mathbf{h}}^{\text{given}}(\gamma) & R < \gamma \leq P \\
\frac{1}{2} g_{\mathbf{h}}^{\text{given}}(\gamma - P) & P < \gamma \leq P + R,
\end{cases}
$$

125

where $g_{\mathbf{h}}^{\text{given}}(\gamma) = (P + P_0) p_{\mathbf{h}}^{\text{given}}(\gamma)$ and $g_{\mathbf{h}}(\gamma) = (P + P_0) p_{\mathbf{h}}(\gamma)$. The newly introduced scaling factor of $\frac{P+R}{P}$ must be taken into account in our implementation of splitting steepest descent (Alg. 1), which gives Alg. (2), shown below.

```
Algorithm 2 Rescaled splitting steepest descent [87, 88]
 1: Preallocate space for a DAM with $P_{\text{max}}$ hidden units and the corresponding weights $\mathbf{w}$ and $\mathbf{g}$
 2: Initialize the weights $\mathbf{w}^\mu$ and $\mathbf{g}^\mu$ connected to the $P_{\text{cur}}$ first hidden units $\mu \in \{1, ..., P_{\text{cur}}\}$, as well as $\mathbf{g}^0$
 3: $\min L\left(\mathbf{w}, \mathbf{g}\right)$ with SGD
 4: while $P_{\text{cur}} < P_{\text{max}}$ do
 5:    Identify a subset $\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{copy}} \subseteq \{1, ..., P_{\text{cur}}\}$ of $R \leq P_{\text{max}} - P_{\text{cur}}$ hidden units to split, return if empty
 6:    Let $\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{paste}} = \{P_{\text{cur}} + 1, ..., P_{\text{cur}} + R\}$ and $\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{dupli}} = \{1, ..., P_{\text{cur}} + R\}$
 7:    Build weights $\mathbf{w}^{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{paste}}} = \mathbf{w}^{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{copy}}}$ for $\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{paste}}$
 8:    Rescale $\mathbf{g}^{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{copy}}} \leftarrow \mathbf{g}^{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{copy}}}/2$ and $g_{\mathbf{h}}\left(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{split}}\right) \leftarrow g_{\mathbf{h}}\left(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{split}}\right)/2$
 9:    Build weights $\mathbf{g}^{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{paste}}} = \mathbf{g}^{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{copy}}}$ and $g\left(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{paste}}\right) = g\left(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{copy}}\right)$ for $\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{paste}}$
10:    Rescale $\mathbf{g}^{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{dupli}}} \leftarrow \frac{P_{\text{cur}}+R}{P_{\text{cur}}} \mathbf{g}^{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{dupli}}}$ and $g_{\mathbf{h}}\left(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{dupli}}\right) \leftarrow \frac{P_{\text{cur}}+R}{P_{\text{cur}}} g_{\mathbf{h}}\left(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\text{dupli}}\right)$
11:    Update $P_{\text{cur}} \leftarrow P_{\text{cur}} + R$
12:    Escape the saddle point by $2^{\text{nd}}$ order descent of $L\left(\mathbf{w}, \mathbf{g}\right)$ w.r.t. $\mathbf{w}$
13:    $\min L\left(\mathbf{w}, \mathbf{g}\right)$ with SGD
14: end while
15: return                     See Appendix 4.K and [87, 88] for details about steps 5 and 12
```

We initialize the weights $\mathbf{g}^\gamma$ and the hidden unit distribution $g_{\mathbf{h}}(\gamma)$ according to

$$
g_y^\gamma =
\begin{cases}
P_0 p_{\mathbf{q}}(y) & \gamma = 0 \\
p_{\mathbf{q}}(y) & \gamma > 0
\end{cases}
$$

$$
\text{and } \quad
g_{\mathbf{h}}(\gamma) =
\begin{cases}
\frac{P_0}{P+P_0} & \gamma = 0 \\
\frac{1}{P+P_0} & \gamma > 0.
\end{cases}
$$

When we train our DAM without splitting steepest descent, we set $P_0 = 1$. As discussed in [236, 237], the resulting weights are, in some sense, the most "typical" for the constraints $g_y^\gamma \geq 0$, $\sum_{\gamma=0}^P g_y^\gamma = (P+1) p_{\mathbf{q}}(y)$ and $\sum_{y=0}^C g_y^\gamma = (P+1) p_{\mathbf{h}}(\gamma) = 1$ (see Section 4.2). With splitting steepest descent, we set $P_0 = P_{\text{cur}}/P_{\text{final}}$ to ensure that $g_{\mathbf{h}}(0) = \sum_{y=0}^C g_y^0$ is approximately the same size as the other entries of $g_{\mathbf{h}}(\gamma)$ after training. For simplicity, we set $p_{\mathbf{q}}(y)$ to the proportions of classes in the data during supervised training. For unsupervised training (see Section 4.4.2), we break the permutation symmetry between the different classes by using different values for all the entries of $p_{\mathbf{q}}(y)$. Otherwise, class weights with unbroken permutation symmetries stay stuck at their initial conditions.

126

### 4.F Stationarity conditions of the loss

Since we constrain the memories $\mathbf{w}^\mu$ to have unit norm (see Section 4.2), the method of Lagrange multipliers tells us any set of memories $\mathbf{w}$ that minimizes Eq. (4.4) must solve the extremization problem

$$
\underset{\mathbf{w},\varphi}{\mathrm{Extr}} \left\{ L\left(\mathbf{w},\mathbf{p}\right) + \frac{1}{2} \sum_{\mu=1}^P \varphi^\mu \left( \sum_{i=1}^N [w_i^\mu]^2 - 1 \right) \right\}.
$$

127

The extrema are the points where the gradient vanishes, so they take the form

$$
\partial_{w_{i}^{\mu}} L(\mathbf{w}, \mathbf{p})+\varphi^{\mu} w_{i}^{\mu}=0
$$

$$
\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left[w_{i}^{\mu}\right]^{2}=1
$$

for all $1 \leq \mu \leq P$. We calculate $\partial_{w_{i}^{\mu}} L(\mathbf{w}, \mathbf{p})$ and write the solution in the more explicit form

$$
\bar{w}_{i}^{\mu}=\sum_{\mu_{*}=1}^{P^{*}} x_{i}^{* \mu_{*}} \sum_{y=0}^{C} q_{y}^{* \mu_{*}} \sigma_{\mu}\left(\beta m^{\mu_{*}}\left(\mathbf{x}^{*}, \mathbf{w}\right)+\log \left[\mathbf{p}_{y}\right]\right)
$$

$$
w_{i}^{\mu}=\frac{\bar{w}_{i}^{\mu}}{\sqrt{\sum_{j=1}^{N}\left[\bar{w}_{j}^{\mu}\right]^{2}}},
$$

for all $1 \leq \mu \leq P$, where $\sigma_{\gamma}\left(x^{\gamma_{*}}\right)=\frac{\exp \left(x^{\gamma_{*} \gamma}\right)}{\sum_{\nu=0}^{P} \exp \left(x^{\gamma_{*} \nu}\right)}, m^{\mu_{*} \mu}\left(\mathbf{x}^{*}, \mathbf{w}\right)=\sum_{i=1}^{N} x_{i}^{* \mu_{*}} w_{i}^{\mu}$ for $1 \leq \mu \leq P$ and $m^{\mu_{*} 0}(\mathbf{x}, \mathbf{w})=\frac{1}{\beta} \log \left[\Omega_{N}(\beta) / \Omega_{N}(0)\right]$.

Similarly, any set of class weights $\mathbf{p}$ that minimizes Eq. (4.4) must solve the extremization problem

$$
\underset{\mathbf{p}, \omega, \lambda}{\operatorname{Extr}}\left\{L(\mathbf{w}, \mathbf{p})+\sum_{y=0}^{C} \lambda_{y}\left(\sum_{\gamma=0}^{P} p_{y}^{\gamma}-p_{\mathbf{q}}(y)\right)+\sum_{\gamma=0}^{P} \omega^{\gamma}\left(\sum_{y=0}^{C} p_{y}^{\gamma}-p_{\mathbf{h}}(\gamma)\right)\right\}.
$$

In Appendix 4.C, we show that its solution is

$$
\bar{p}_{y}^{\gamma}=\sum_{\mu_{*}=1}^{P^{*}} q_{y}^{* \mu_{*}} \sigma_{\gamma}\left(\beta m^{\mu_{*}}(\mathbf{x}, \mathbf{w})+\log \left[\mathbf{p}_{y}\right]\right)
$$

$$
p_{y}^{\gamma}=\frac{\bar{p}_{y}^{\gamma}}{\zeta_{y}^{\gamma}\left(\overline{\mathbf{p}} ; p_{\mathbf{h}}\right)}
$$

for all $0 \leq \gamma \leq P$, where the normalization constant $\zeta_{y}^{\gamma}\left(\overline{\mathbf{p}} ; p_{\mathbf{h}}\right)$ is defined using Eqs. (4.21) of Appendix 4.C.

Combining these equations with the ones for $\mathbf{w}$, we find the stationarity conditions

$$
\bar{w}_{i}^{\mu}=\sum_{\mu_{*}=1}^{P^{*}} x_{i}^{* \mu_{*}} \sum_{y=0}^{C} q_{y}^{* \mu_{*}} \sigma_{\mu}\left(\beta m^{\mu_{*}}\left(\mathbf{x}^{*}, \mathbf{w}\right)+\log \left[\mathbf{p}_{y}\right]\right)
$$

$$
\bar{p}_{y}^{\gamma}=\sum_{\mu_{*}=1}^{P^{*}} q_{y}^{* \mu_{*}} \sigma_{\gamma}\left(\beta m^{\mu_{*}}\left(\mathbf{x}^{*}, \mathbf{w}\right)+\log \left[\mathbf{p}_{y}\right]\right)
$$

$$
w_{i}^{\mu}=\frac{\bar{w}_{i}^{\mu}}{\sqrt{\sum_{j=1}^{N}\left[\bar{w}_{j}^{\mu}\right]^{2}}}
$$

$$
p_{y}^{\gamma}=\frac{\bar{p}_{y}^{\gamma}}{\zeta_{y}^{\gamma}\left(\overline{\mathbf{p}} ; p_{\mathbf{h}}\right)}
$$

for all $1 \leq \mu \leq P$ and $0 \leq \gamma \leq P$.

### 4.G Replicated partition function and free entropy

Suppose a student DAM model is trained using a dataset $\mathcal{D} = \{\mathbf{x}^c, y^c\}_{c=1}^M$ of $M$ i.i.d. examples $\mathbf{x}^c$ with labels $y^c$. By Bayes' theorem, the student weights $\mathbf{w}$ and $\mathbf{p}$ follow the distribution

$$
\mathrm{P}_\beta(\mathbf{w}, \mathbf{p}|\mathcal{D}) = \mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{D})^{-1} \mathrm{P}(\mathbf{w}, \mathbf{p}) \prod_{c=1}^M \mathrm{P}_\beta(\mathbf{x}^c, y^c|\mathbf{w}, \mathbf{p}),
$$

where $\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{D}) = \mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{w},\mathbf{p}} \left[\prod_{c=1}^M \mathrm{P}_\beta(\mathbf{x}^c, y^c|\mathbf{w}, \mathbf{p})\right]$. Assuming the examples are sampled from a teacher DAM with weights $\mathbf{w}^*$ and $\mathbf{p}^*$, the average replicated partition takes the form

$$
\begin{aligned}
\langle \mathcal{Z}^L \rangle &= \sum_{\{y^c\}_{c=1}^M} \int \left[\prod_{c=1}^M d\mathbf{x}^c\right] \mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{w}^*,\mathbf{p}^*} \left[\prod_{c=1}^M \mathrm{P}_\beta(\mathbf{x}^c, y^c|\mathbf{w}^*, \mathbf{p}^*)\right] \mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{D})^L \\
&= \sum_{\{y^c\}_{c=1}^M} \int \left[\prod_{c=1}^M d\mathbf{x}^c\right] \mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{w}^*,\mathbf{p}^*} \left[\prod_{c=1}^M \mathrm{P}_\beta(\mathbf{x}^c, y^c|\mathbf{w}^*, \mathbf{p}^*)\right] \prod_{a=1}^L \mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{w}^a,\mathbf{p}^a} \left[\prod_{c=1}^M \mathrm{P}_\beta(\mathbf{x}^c, y^c|\mathbf{w}^a, \mathbf{p}^a)\right] \\
&= \mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{w}^*,\mathbf{w}} \mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{p}^*,\mathbf{p}} \left[\prod_{c=1}^M \sum_{y^c=0}^C \int_{S^{N-1}} d\mathbf{x}^c \mathrm{P}_\beta(\mathbf{x}^c, y^c|\mathbf{w}^*, \mathbf{p}^*) \prod_{a=1}^L \mathrm{P}_\beta(\mathbf{x}^c, y^c|\mathbf{w}^a, \mathbf{p}^a)\right] \\
&= \mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{w}^*,\mathbf{w}} \mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{p}^*,\mathbf{p}} \left[\left(\sum_{y=0}^C \int_{S^{N-1}} d\mathbf{x} \mathrm{P}_\beta(\mathbf{x}, y|\mathbf{w}^*, \mathbf{p}^*) \prod_{a=1}^L \mathrm{P}_\beta(\mathbf{x}, y|\mathbf{w}^a, \mathbf{p}^a)\right)^M\right],
\end{aligned}
$$

where we redefined $\mathbf{w} = \{\mathbf{w}^a\}_{a=1}^L$ and $\mathbf{p} = \{\mathbf{p}^a\}_{a=1}^L$. In order to simplify the argument of $(\cdot)^M$, it is convenient to write Eq. (4.3) in the form

$$
\mathrm{P}_\beta(\mathbf{x}, y|\mathbf{w}^a, \mathbf{p}^a) = \sum_{\gamma=0}^P \Omega_N (\beta [1 - \delta_{\gamma 0}])^{-1} p_y^{a\gamma} \exp \left( \beta [1 - \delta_{\gamma 0}] \sum_{i=1}^N w_i^{a\gamma} x_i \right), \tag{4.23}
$$

where the value of $\mathbf{w}^{a0}$ is arbitrary. Until the end of this Section, all sums over the hidden units will include the index 0 unless explicitly indicated otherwise. Define $I_y(\mathbf{x}) = \mathrm{P}_\beta(\mathbf{x}, y|\mathbf{w}^*, \mathbf{p}^*) \prod_{a=1}^L \mathrm{P}_\beta(\mathbf{x}, y|\mathbf{w}^a, \mathbf{p}^a)$, then

$$
\begin{aligned}
I_y(\mathbf{x}) &= \left[\sum_{\gamma_*=0}^{P^*} \Omega_N (\beta^* [1 - \delta_{\gamma_* 0}])^{-1} p_y^{* \gamma_*} \exp \left( \beta^* [1 - \delta_{\gamma_* 0}] \sum_{i=1}^N w_i^{* \gamma_*} x_i \right)\right] \\
&\quad \prod_{a=1}^L \left[\sum_{\gamma=0}^P \Omega_N (\beta [1 - \delta_{\gamma 0}])^{-1} p_y^{a\gamma} \exp \left( \beta [1 - \delta_{\gamma 0}] \sum_{i=1}^N w_i^{a\gamma} x_i \right)\right] \\
&= \sum_{\gamma_* \gamma_1 \cdots \gamma_L} \Omega_N (\beta^* [1 - \delta_{\gamma_* 0}])^{-1} \left[\prod_a \Omega_N (\beta [1 - \delta_{\gamma_a 0}])\right]^{-1} \\
&\quad p_y^{* \gamma_*} \left[\prod_a p_y^{a \gamma_a}\right] \exp \left( \beta^* [1 - \delta_{\gamma_* 0}] \sum_i w_i^{* \gamma_*} x_i + \beta \sum_a [1 - \delta_{\gamma_a 0}] \sum_i w_i^{a \gamma_a} x_i \right).
\end{aligned}
$$

129

We will now evaluate the integral $\int_{S^{N-1}} dx\ I_y(\mathbf{x})$. Using Eq. (4.18) of Appendix 4.B with $\rho = \rho_{\gamma_*\gamma} := \frac{1}{N}\sqrt{\sum_i \left[\beta^* [1-\delta_{\gamma_*0}] w_i^{*\gamma_*} + \beta \sum_a [1-\delta_{\gamma_a0}] w_i^{a\gamma_a}\right]^2}$, we get

$$
\begin{aligned}
\int_{S^{N-1}} dx \exp\left(\beta^* [1-\delta_{\gamma_*0}] \sum_i w_i^{*\gamma_*} x_i + \beta \sum_a [1-\delta_{\gamma_a0}] \sum_i w_i^{a\gamma_a} x_i\right)
&\approx \Omega_N(0)\left(1+(2\rho_{\gamma_*\gamma})^2\right)^{-1/4} \exp\left[\left(\frac{N}{2}-1\right)\eta(2\rho_{\gamma_*\gamma})\right],
\end{aligned}
$$

in the limit of large $N$. Since $\sqrt{\sum_i (w_i^{*\gamma_*})^2}=1$, the square of $\rho_{\gamma_*\gamma}$ simplifies to

$$
\begin{aligned}
(\rho_{\gamma_*\gamma})^2 &= \left(\frac{1}{N}\sqrt{\sum_i \left[\beta^* [1-\delta_{\gamma_*0}] w_i^{*\gamma_*} + \beta \sum_a [1-\delta_{\gamma_a0}] w_i^{a\gamma_a}\right]^2}\right)^2 \\
&= v^2 [1-\delta_{\gamma_*0}] + 2\frac{\beta}{N}v [1-\delta_{\gamma_*0}] \sum_a [1-\delta_{\gamma_a0}] \sum_i w_i^{*\gamma_*} w_i^{a\gamma_a} \\
&\quad + \frac{\beta^2}{N^2} \sum_{a,b} [1-\delta_{\gamma_a0}] [1-\delta_{\gamma_b0}] \sum_i w_i^{a\gamma_a} w_i^{b\gamma_b},
\end{aligned}
$$

where $v = \frac{\beta^*}{N}$. To leading order in $\frac{\beta}{N}$ we find

$$
\begin{aligned}
\left(1+(2\rho_{\gamma_*\gamma})^2\right)^{-1/4} &\approx \left(1+\left(2v [1-\delta_{\gamma_*0}]\right)^2\right)^{-1/4} \\
\text{and}\quad \left(\frac{N}{2}-1\right)\eta(2\rho_{\gamma_*\gamma_a}) &\approx \left(\frac{N}{2}-1\right)\eta(2v [1-\delta_{\gamma_*0}]) + \beta_{\text{eff}} [1-\delta_{\gamma_*0}] \sum_a [1-\delta_{\gamma_a0}] \sum_i w_i^{*\gamma_*} w_i^{a\gamma_a} \\
&\quad + \frac{[\beta \xi_{\gamma_*}]^2}{2N} \sum_{a,b} [1-\delta_{\gamma_a0}] [1-\delta_{\gamma_b0}] \sum_i w_i^{a\gamma_a} w_i^{b\gamma_b},
\end{aligned}
$$

where $\beta_{\text{eff}} = \frac{2v}{\sqrt{[2v]^2+1}+1}\beta$ and $\xi_{\gamma_*} = \delta_{\gamma_*0} + \sqrt{\frac{2}{\sqrt{[2v]^2+1}+1}} [1-\delta_{\gamma_*0}]$. Assuming that $v \gg 1/N$ and $\beta \ll N$, we drop the last term. Finally, we use Eq. (4.18) backwards with $\rho = v [1-\delta_{\gamma_*0}]$ and obtain

$$
\begin{aligned}
\Omega_N\left(\beta^* [1-\delta_{\gamma_*0}]\right)^{-1} \int_{S^{N-1}} dx \exp\left(\beta^* [1-\delta_{\gamma_*0}] \sum_i w_i^{*\gamma_*} x_i + \beta \sum_a [1-\delta_{\gamma_a0}] \sum_i w_i^{a\gamma_a} x_i\right)
&\approx \exp\left(\beta_{\text{eff}} [1-\delta_{\gamma_*0}] \sum_a [1-\delta_{\gamma_a0}] \sum_i w_i^{*\gamma_*} w_i^{a\gamma_a}\right),
\end{aligned}
$$

from which we conclude that

$$
\begin{aligned}
\sum_{y=0}^C \int_{S^{N-1}} dx\ I_y(\mathbf{x}) &\approx \sum_{\gamma_*\gamma_1\cdots\gamma_L} \sum_y p_y^{*\gamma_*} \left[\prod_a p_y^{a\gamma_a}\right] \left[\prod_a \Omega_N\left(\beta [1-\delta_{\gamma_a0}]\right)\right]^{-1} \\
&\quad \exp\left(\beta_{\text{eff}} [1-\delta_{\gamma_*0}] \sum_a [1-\delta_{\gamma_a0}] \sum_i w_i^{*\gamma_*} w_i^{a\gamma_a}\right).
\end{aligned}
$$

130

We define $\alpha = M/N$, introduce the order parameter
$$
m^{a \gamma_{*} \nu} \text{ for } \sum_{i} w_{i}^{* \gamma_{*}} w_{i}^{a \nu}, \tag{4.24}
$$
and insert into $\langle\mathcal{Z}^{L}\rangle$ the identity operator
$$
\begin{aligned}
1 &=\int_{\mathbb{R}} \prod_{\gamma_{*}, \nu ; a} d m^{a \gamma_{*} \nu} \delta\left(m^{a \gamma_{*} \nu}-\sum_{i} w_{i}^{* \gamma_{*}} w_{i}^{a \nu}\right) \\
&=\int_{\mathbb{R}} \prod_{\gamma_{*}, \nu ; a} d m^{a \gamma_{*} \nu} \int_{i \mathbb{R}} \prod_{\gamma_{*}, \nu ; a} d \hat{m}^{a \gamma_{*} \nu} \exp \left\{\frac{\beta_{\mathrm{eff}} \alpha}{P_{*}} \sum_{\gamma_{*}, \nu ; a} \hat{m}^{a \gamma_{*} \nu}\left(\sum_{i} w_{i}^{* \gamma_{*}} w_{i}^{a \nu}-m^{a \gamma_{*} \nu}\right)\right\}
\end{aligned}
$$
so that we can rewrite it as
$$
\begin{aligned}
\left\langle\mathcal{Z}^{L}\right\rangle= & \int \prod_{\gamma_{*}, \nu ; a} d \hat{m}^{a \gamma_{*} \nu} d m^{a \gamma_{*} \nu} \mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{w}^{*}, \mathbf{w}} \exp \left\{N H_{S}\left(\mathbf{w}, \mathbf{w}^{*} ; \hat{\mathbf{m}}\right)\right\} \exp \left\{-N H_{Q}(\mathbf{m}, \hat{\mathbf{m}})\right\} \\
& \mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{p}^{*}, \mathbf{p}} \exp \left\{\alpha N \log \left[\sum_{\gamma_{*} \gamma_{1} \cdots \gamma_{L}} \sum_{y} p_{y}^{* \gamma_{*}}\left[\prod_{a} p_{y}^{a \gamma_{a}}\right] \exp \left\{H_{E}\left(\gamma, \gamma_{*} ; \mathbf{m}\right)\right\}\right]\right\},
\end{aligned} \tag{4.25}
$$
where $\quad H_{S}\left(\mathbf{w}, \mathbf{w}^{*} ; \hat{\mathbf{m}}\right)=\frac{\beta_{\mathrm{eff}} \alpha}{P_{*}} \sum_{\gamma_{*}, \nu ; a} \hat{m}^{a \gamma_{*} \nu} \sum_{i} w_{i}^{* \gamma_{*}} w_{i}^{a \nu}$

$$
H_{Q}(\mathbf{m}, \hat{\mathbf{m}})=\frac{\beta_{\mathrm{eff}} \alpha}{P_{*}} \sum_{\gamma_{*}, \nu ; a} \hat{m}^{a \gamma_{*} \nu} m^{a \gamma_{*} \nu},
$$

$$
H_{E}\left(\gamma, \gamma_{*} ; \mathbf{m}\right)=-\sum_{a} \log \left[\Omega_{N}\left(\beta\left[1-\delta_{\gamma_{a} 0}\right]\right)\right]+\beta_{\mathrm{eff}}\left[1-\delta_{\gamma_{*} 0}\right] \sum_{a}\left[1-\delta_{\gamma_{a} 0}\right] m^{a \gamma_{*} \gamma_{a}}.
$$

Using Eq. (4.18) of Appendix 4.B, the exponential of $H_{S}$ integrates to
$$
\begin{aligned}
& \mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{w}} \exp \left\{N H_{S}\left(\mathbf{w}, \mathbf{w}^{*} ; \hat{\mathbf{m}}\right)\right\} \\
&= \Omega_{N}(0)^{-P L} \int_{S^{N-1}} d \mathbf{w} \exp \left(\frac{\beta_{\mathrm{eff}} \alpha}{P_{*}} \sum_{\gamma_{*}, \nu ; a} \hat{m}^{a \gamma_{*} \nu} \sum_{i} w_{i}^{* \gamma_{*}} w_{i}^{a \nu}\right) \\
& \approx \prod_{\nu ; a} \exp \left[-\frac{1}{4} \log \left(1+\left[\frac{2 \beta_{\mathrm{eff}} \alpha}{P_{*}}\right]^{2} \sum_{i}\left[\sum_{\gamma_{*}} \hat{m}^{a \gamma_{*} \nu} w_{i}^{* \gamma_{*}}\right]^{2}\right)\right] \\
& \quad \exp \left[\left(\frac{N}{2}-1\right) \eta\left(\frac{2 \beta_{\mathrm{eff}} \alpha}{P_{*}} \sqrt{\sum_{i}\left[\sum_{\gamma_{*}} \hat{m}^{a \gamma_{*} \nu} w_{i}^{* \gamma_{*}}\right]^{2}}\right)\right].
\end{aligned}
$$

In order to continue the calculations, we make the replica-symmetric ansatz
$$
m^{a \gamma_{*} \nu}=m^{\gamma_{*} \nu} \text{ and } \hat{m}^{a \gamma_{*} \nu}=\hat{m}^{\gamma_{*} \nu} \text{ for all } a ; \gamma_{*}, \nu
$$

$$
p_{y}^{a \nu}=p_{y}^{\nu} \text{ for all } a ; \nu
$$

so that we can use the replica trick to simplify

$$
\begin{aligned}
& \frac{1}{L} \log \left[\sum_{\gamma_{*} \gamma_{1} \ldots \gamma_{L}} \sum_{y} p_{y}^{* \gamma_{*}}\left[\prod_{a} p_{y}^{a \gamma_{a}}\right] \exp \left\{H_{E}\left(\gamma, \gamma_{*} ; \mathbf{m}\right)\right\}\right] \\
& =\frac{1}{L} \log \left[\sum_{\gamma_{*}} \sum_{y} p_{y}^{* \gamma_{*}} \sum_{\gamma_{1} \ldots \gamma_{L}}\left[\prod_{a} p_{y}^{a \gamma_{a}}\right] \exp \left\{H_{E}\left(\gamma, \gamma_{*} ; \mathbf{m}\right)\right\}\right] \\
& \approx \sum_{\gamma_{*}} \sum_{y} p_{y}^{* \gamma_{*}} \log \left[\sum_{\gamma} p_{y}^{\gamma} \Omega_{N}\left(\beta\left[1-\delta_{\gamma 0}\right]\right)^{-1} \exp \left(\beta_{\mathrm{eff}}\left[1-\delta_{\gamma_{*} 0}\right]\left[1-\delta_{\gamma 0}\right] m^{\gamma_{*} \gamma}\right)\right],
\end{aligned}
$$

as we take $L$ to zero. Similarly, we get

$$
\begin{aligned}
\frac{1}{L} \log \left[\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{w}} \exp \left\{N H_{S}\left(\mathbf{w}, \mathbf{w}^{*} ; \hat{\mathbf{m}}\right)\right\}\right] & =-\frac{1}{4} \log \left(1+\left[\frac{2 \beta_{\mathrm{eff}} \alpha}{P^{*}}\right]^{2} \sum_{i}\left[\sum_{\gamma_{*}} \hat{m}^{\gamma_{*} \nu} w_{i}^{* \gamma_{*}}\right]^{2}\right) \\
& +\left(\frac{N}{2}-1\right) \eta\left(\frac{2 \beta_{\mathrm{eff}} \alpha}{P^{*}} \sqrt{\sum_{i}\left[\sum_{\gamma_{*}} \hat{m}^{\gamma_{*} \nu} w_{i}^{* \gamma_{*}}\right]^{2}}\right).
\end{aligned}
$$

From now on, we assume for simplicity that $\sum_{y} p_{y}^{* 0}=p_{\mathbf{h}}^{*}(0)=0$ and $\sum_{y} p_{y}^{* \mu_{*}}=p_{\mathbf{h}}^{*}\left(\mu_{*}\right)=1 / P^{*}$ for all $\mu_{*}>0$. Defining $g_{y}^{* \mu_{*}}=P^{*} p_{y}^{* \mu_{*}}=\mathrm{P}_{\beta}\left(y \mid \mu_{*}, \mathbf{J}\right)$ for all $\mu_{*}>0, \mathbf{g}^{*}=\left\{g_{y}^{* \gamma_{*}}\right\}_{0 \leq y \leq C}^{1 \leq \mu_{*} \leq P^{*}}$ and $\varrho=\frac{\alpha}{P^{*}}=\frac{M}{P^{*} N}$, the free entropy $f$ then takes the form

$$
f \approx \underset{\mathbf{m}, \hat{\mathbf{m}}, \mathbf{p}}{\operatorname{Extr}}\{f(\mathbf{m}, \hat{\mathbf{m}}, \mathbf{p})\} \quad \text { such that } \quad \sum_{\gamma=0}^{P} p_{y}^{\gamma}=p_{\mathbf{q}}(y) \quad \text { and } \quad \sum_{y=0}^{C} p_{y}^{\gamma}=p_{\mathbf{h}}(\gamma)
\tag{4.26}
$$

$$
\begin{aligned}
\text { with } \quad f(\mathbf{m}, \hat{\mathbf{m}}, \mathbf{p}) & =-\beta_{\mathrm{eff}} \varrho \sum_{\gamma_{*}, \gamma=0}^{P^{*}, P} \hat{m}^{\gamma_{*} \gamma} m^{\gamma_{*} \gamma}+\frac{1}{2} \mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{w}^{*}}\left[\sum_{\gamma=0}^{P} \eta\left(2 \beta_{\mathrm{eff}} \varrho \sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left[\sum_{\gamma_{*}=0}^{P^{*}} \hat{m}^{\gamma_{*} \gamma} w_{i}^{* \gamma_{*}}\right]^{2}}\right)\right] \\
& +\varrho \sum_{\mu_{*}=1}^{P^{*}} \sum_{y=0}^{C} \mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{g}^{*}}\left[g_{y}^{* \mu_{*}}\right] \log \left[\sum_{\gamma=0}^{P} p_{y}^{\gamma} \Omega_{N}\left(\beta\left[1-\delta_{\gamma 0}\right]\right)^{-1} \exp \left(\beta_{\mathrm{eff}}\left[1-\delta_{\gamma 0}\right] m^{\mu_{*} \gamma}\right)\right]
\end{aligned}
$$

where we approximated the expectation over $\mathbf{p}$ as a (constrained) extremization problem using Laplace's method. By inspection, the order parameters $m^{0 \gamma}$ and $\hat{m}^{0 \gamma}$ always vanish, so we ignore them in the incoming derivation of the saddle-point equations.

### 4.H Saddle-point equations

In this Appendix, we adopt the convention $1 \leq \mu_{*} \leq P^{*}, 0 \leq \gamma \leq P$ and $1 \leq \mu \leq P$. By the Lagrange multiplier theorem, any set of class weights $\mathbf{p}$ that extremizes Eq. (4.26) must solve the extremization problem

$$
\underset{\mathbf{p}, \omega, \lambda}{\operatorname{Extr}}\left\{f(\mathbf{m}, \hat{\mathbf{m}}, \mathbf{p})+\sum_{y=0}^{C} \lambda_{y}\left(\sum_{\gamma=0}^{P} p_{y}^{\gamma}-p_{\mathbf{q}}(y)\right)+\sum_{\gamma=0}^{P} \omega^{\gamma}\left(\sum_{y=0}^{C} p_{y}^{\gamma}-p_{\mathbf{h}}(\gamma)\right)\right\}.
$$


In Appendix 4.C, we show that its solution is

$$
\bar{p}_{y}^{\gamma}=\sum_{\mu_{*}=1}^{P^{*}} \mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{g}^{*}}\left[g_{y}^{* \mu_{*}}\right] \sigma_{\gamma}\left(\beta_{\mathrm{eff}}\left[1-\delta_{\gamma 0}\right] m^{\mu_{*}}-\log \left[\Omega_{N}\left(\beta\left[1-\delta_{\gamma 0}\right]\right)\right]+\log \left[\mathbf{p}_{y}\right]\right)
$$

$$
p_{y}^{\gamma}=\frac{\bar{p}_{y}^{\gamma}}{\zeta_{y}^{\gamma}\left(\overline{\mathbf{p}} ; p_{\mathbf{h}}\right)},
$$

for all $0 \leq \gamma \leq P$, where $\zeta_{y}^{\gamma}\left(\overline{\mathbf{p}} ; p_{\mathbf{h}}\right)$ is defined using Eqs. (4.21) of Appendix 4.C and $\sigma_{\gamma}\left(x^{\mu_{*}}\right)=$ $\frac{\exp \left(x^{\mu_{*} \gamma}\right)}{\sum_{\kappa=0}^{P} \exp \left(x^{\mu_{*} \kappa}\right)}$ is the softmax function. We extremize Eq. (4.26) with respect to the remaining parameters by solving for the gradient equal to zero. $\partial_{m^{\mu_{*} \mu}} f(\mathbf{m}, \hat{\mathbf{m}}, \mathbf{p})=0$ immediately yields

$$
\hat{m}^{\mu_{*} \gamma}=\left[1-\delta_{\gamma 0}\right] \sum_{y=0}^{C} \mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{g}^{*}}\left[g_{y}^{* \mu_{*}}\right] \frac{p_{y}^{\gamma} \Omega_{N}\left(\beta\left[1-\delta_{\gamma 0}\right]\right)^{-1} \exp \left(\beta_{\mathrm{eff}}\left[1-\delta_{\gamma 0}\right] m^{\mu_{*} \gamma}\right)}{\sum_{\kappa=0}^{P} p_{y}^{\kappa} \Omega_{N}\left(\beta\left[1-\delta_{\kappa 0}\right]\right)^{-1} \exp \left(\beta_{\mathrm{eff}}\left[1-\delta_{\kappa 0}\right] m^{\mu_{*} \kappa}\right)}. \tag{4.27}
$$

On the other hand, $\partial_{\hat{m}^{\mu_{*} \gamma}} f(\mathbf{m}, \hat{\mathbf{m}}, \mathbf{p})=0$ gives an equation that depends on the choice of prior for the teacher memories $\mathbf{w}^{* \mu_{*}}$. We first investigate the case where the teacher memories $\mathbf{w}^{* \mu_{*}}$ and the class weights $\mathbf{g}^{*}$ are distributed uniformly at random over the sets to which they are constrained (see Section 4.2.2). In this scenario, we have $\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{g}^{*}}\left[g_{y}^{* \mu_{*}}\right]=p_{\mathbf{q}}^{*}(y)$. Moreover, assuming that $P^{*} \ll N$, random vectors on the unit sphere are orthonormal with high probability, which means that

$$
\begin{aligned}
\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left[\sum_{\mu_{*}=1}^{P^{*}} \hat{m}^{\mu_{*} \gamma} w_{i}^{* \mu_{*}}\right]^{2} &=\sum_{i=1}^{N} \sum_{\mu_{*}, \nu_{*}=1}^{P^{*}} \hat{m}^{\mu_{*} \gamma} \hat{m}^{\nu_{*} \gamma} w_{i}^{* \mu_{*}} w_{i}^{* \nu_{*}} \\
&=\sum_{\mu_{*}=1}^{P^{*}}\left[\hat{m}^{\mu_{*} \gamma}\right]^{2}+\sum_{\mu_{*} \neq \nu_{*}} \hat{m}^{\mu_{*} \gamma} \hat{m}^{\nu_{*} \gamma} \sum_{i=1}^{N} w_{i}^{* \mu_{*}} w_{i}^{* \nu_{*}} \\
& \approx \sum_{\mu_{*}=1}^{P^{*}}\left[\hat{m}^{\mu_{*} \gamma}\right]^{2}.
\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore, $\partial_{\hat{m}^{\mu_{*} \gamma}} f(\mathbf{m}, \hat{\mathbf{m}}, \mathbf{p})=0$ gives

$$
0=\partial_{\hat{m}^{\mu_{*} \gamma}} f(\mathbf{m}, \hat{\mathbf{m}}, \mathbf{p})
$$

$$
0=-\beta_{\mathrm{eff}} \varrho m^{\mu_{*} \gamma}+\frac{1}{2} \partial_{\hat{m}^{\mu_{*} \gamma}} \sum_{\kappa=0}^{P} \eta\left(2 \beta_{\mathrm{eff}} \varrho \sqrt{\sum_{\nu_{*}=1}^{P^{*}}\left[\hat{m}^{\nu_{*} \kappa}\right]^{2}}\right)
$$

$$
m^{\mu_{*} \gamma}=\varsigma\left(2 \beta_{\mathrm{eff}} \varrho \sqrt{\sum_{\nu_{*}=1}^{P^{*}}\left[\hat{m}^{\nu_{*} \gamma}\right]^{2}}\right) \frac{\hat{m}^{\mu_{*} \gamma}}{\sqrt{\sum_{\nu_{*}=1}^{P^{*}}\left[\hat{m}^{\nu_{*} \gamma}\right]^{2}}},
$$

where $\varsigma(x)=\frac{x}{\sqrt{x^{2}+1}+1}$. $\hat{m}^{\mu_{*} \gamma}$ vanishes (see Eqs. 4.27) and $m^{\mu_{*} \gamma}$ is arbitrary (see Eqs. 4.23 and 4.24) when $\gamma=0$. Therefore, we may update only $\hat{m}^{\mu_{*} \mu}$ and $m^{\mu_{*} \mu}$ with $1 \leq \mu \leq P$ when solving for $\nabla f=0$ by

fixed-point iteration. Defining $m^{\mu_{*} 0}=\frac{1}{\beta_{\text {eff }}} \log \left[\Omega_{N}(\beta) / \Omega_{N}(0)\right]$, we then obtain the saddle-point equations

$$
\hat{m}^{\mu_{*} \mu}=\sum_{y=0}^{C} p_{\mathbf{q}}^{*}(y) \sigma_{\mu}\left(\beta_{\mathrm{eff}} m^{\mu_{*}}+\log \left[\mathbf{p}_{y}\right]\right)
$$

$$
m^{\mu_{*} \mu}=\varsigma\left(2 \beta_{\mathrm{eff}} \varrho \sqrt{\sum_{\nu_{*}=1}^{P^{*}}\left[\hat{m}^{\nu_{*} \mu}\right]^{2}}\right) \frac{\hat{m}^{\mu_{*} \mu}}{\sqrt{\sum_{\nu_{*}=1}^{P^{*}}\left[\hat{m}^{\nu_{*} \mu}\right]^{2}}},
$$

for all $1 \leq \mu \leq P$, where we simplified the argument of the softmax function $\sigma_{\mu}\left(x^{\mu_{*}}\right)=\frac{\exp \left(x^{\mu_{*} \mu}\right)}{\sum_{\kappa=0}^{P} \exp \left(x^{\mu_{*} \kappa}\right)}$ using $m^{\mu_{*} 0}=\frac{1}{\beta_{\text {eff }}} \log \left[\Omega_{N}(\beta) / \Omega_{N}(0)\right]$. Putting the equations for $m^{\mu_{*} \mu}$ and $p_{y}^{\gamma}$ together, we find

$$
\hat{m}^{\mu_{*} \mu}=\sum_{y=0}^{C} p_{\mathbf{q}}^{*}(y) \sigma_{\mu}\left(\beta_{\mathrm{eff}} m^{\mu_{*}}+\log \left[\mathbf{p}_{y}\right]\right)
$$

$$
\widetilde{p}_{y}^{\gamma}=p_{\mathbf{q}}^{*}(y) \sum_{\mu=1}^{P^{*}} \sigma_{\gamma}\left(\beta_{\mathrm{eff}} m^{\mu_{*}}+\log \left[\mathbf{p}_{y}\right]\right)
$$

$$
m^{\mu_{*} \mu}=\varsigma\left(2 \beta_{\mathrm{eff}} \varrho \sqrt{\sum_{\nu_{*}=1}^{P^{*}}\left[\hat{m}^{\nu_{*} \mu}\right]^{2}}\right) \frac{\hat{m}^{\mu_{*} \mu}}{\sqrt{\sum_{\nu_{*}=1}^{P^{*}}\left[\hat{m}^{\nu_{*} \mu}\right]^{2}}}
$$

$$
p_{y}^{\gamma}=\frac{\widetilde{p}_{y}^{\gamma}}{\zeta_{y}^{\gamma}\left(\overline{\mathbf{p}} ; p_{\mathbf{h}}\right)}
$$

for all $1 \leq \mu \leq P$ and $0 \leq \gamma \leq P$. If we instead clamp $\mathbf{w}^{* \mu_{*}}$ and $\mathbf{g}^{* \mu_{*}}$ to some fixed patterns $\mathbf{x}^{* \mu_{*}}$ and their soft labels $\mathbf{q}^{* \mu_{*}}$, respectively, then the solutions of Eq. (4.26) take the form

$$
\hat{m}^{\mu_{*} \mu}=\sum_{y=0}^{C} q_{y}^{* \mu_{*}} \sigma_{\mu}\left(\beta_{\mathrm{eff}} m^{\mu_{*}}+\log \left[\mathbf{p}_{y}\right]\right)
$$

$$
\widetilde{p}_{y}^{\gamma}=\sum_{\mu_{*}=1}^{P^{*}} q_{y}^{* \mu_{*}} \sigma_{\gamma}\left(\beta_{\mathrm{eff}} m^{\mu_{*}}+\log \left[\mathbf{p}_{y}\right]\right)
$$

$$
m^{\mu_{*} \mu}=\varsigma\left(2 \beta_{\mathrm{eff}} \varrho \sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left[\sum_{\nu_{*}=1}^{P^{*}} \hat{m}^{\nu_{*} \mu} x_{i}^{* \nu_{*}}\right]^{2}}\right) \frac{\sum_{i=1}^{N} x_{i}^{* \nu_{*}} \sum_{\nu_{*}=1}^{P^{*}} \hat{m}^{\nu_{*} \mu} x_{i}^{* \nu_{*}}}{\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left[\sum_{\nu_{*}=1}^{P^{*}} \hat{m}^{\nu_{*} \mu} x_{i}^{* \nu_{*}}\right]^{2}}}
$$

$$
p_{y}^{\gamma}=\frac{\widetilde{p}_{y}^{\gamma}}{\zeta_{y}^{\gamma}\left(\overline{\mathbf{p}} ; p_{\mathbf{h}}\right)}.
$$

Defining $\bar{x}_{i}^{\mu}=\sum_{\mu_{*}} \hat{m}^{\mu_{*} \mu} x_{i}^{* \mu_{*}}$, we thus find

$$
\bar{x}_{i}^{\mu}=\sum_{\mu_{*}=1}^{P^{*}} x_{i}^{* \mu_{*}} \sum_{y=0}^{C} q_{y}^{* \mu_{*}} \sigma_{\mu}\left(\beta_{\mathrm{eff}} m^{\mu_{*}}+\log \left[\mathbf{p}_{y}\right]\right)
$$

$$
\bar{p}_{y}^{\gamma}=\sum_{\mu_{*}=1}^{P^{*}} q_{y}^{* \mu_{*}} \sigma_{\gamma}\left(\beta_{\mathrm{eff}} m^{\mu_{*}}+\log \left[\mathbf{p}_{y}\right]\right)
$$

$$
m^{\mu_{*} \mu}=\varsigma\left(2 \beta_{\mathrm{eff}} \varrho \sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left[\bar{x}_{i}^{\mu}\right]^{2}}\right) \frac{\sum_{i=1}^{N} x_{i}^{* \mu_{*}} \bar{x}_{i}^{\mu}}{\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left[\bar{x}_{i}^{\mu}\right]^{2}}}
$$

$$
p_{y}^{\gamma}=\frac{\bar{p}_{y}^{\gamma}}{\zeta_{y}^{\gamma}\left(\overline{\mathbf{p}} ; p_{\mathbf{h}}\right)},
$$

for all $1 \leq \mu \leq P$ and $0 \leq \gamma \leq P$.

### 4.1 Saddle-point hierarchy
Suppose that the set of parameters $\bar{x}_{i}^{\text {fixed,} \mu}, \bar{p}_{y}^{\text {fixed,} \gamma}, m^{\text {fixed,} \mu_{*} \gamma}, p_{y}^{\text {fixed,} \gamma}$ with hidden unit prior $p_{\mathbf{h}}^{\text {given }}(\gamma)$ is a fixed point of Eqs. (4.10) with $P$ hidden units. Substitute into the same saddle-point equations with $P+R \in\{P, \ldots, 2 P\}$ hidden units the duplicated order parameters

$$
\bar{x}_{i}^{\mathrm{dupli}, \mu}= \begin{cases}\bar{x}_{i}^{\mathrm{fixed}, \mu} & 0<\mu \leq P \\ \bar{x}_{i}^{\mathrm{fixed}, \mu-P} & P<\mu \leq P+R\end{cases}
$$

$$
\bar{p}_{y}^{\mathrm{dupli}, \gamma}= \begin{cases}\bar{p}_{y}^{\mathrm{fixed}, 0} & \gamma=0 \\ \frac{1}{2} \bar{p}_{y}^{\mathrm{fixed}, \gamma} & 0<\gamma \leq R \\ \bar{p}_{y}^{\mathrm{fixed}, \gamma} & R<\gamma \leq P \\ \frac{1}{2} \bar{p}_{y}^{\mathrm{fixed}, \gamma-P} & P<\gamma \leq P+R\end{cases}
$$

$$
m^{\mathrm{dupli}, \mu_{*} \gamma}= \begin{cases}m^{\mathrm{fixed}, \mu_{*} 0} & \gamma=0 \\ m^{\mathrm{fixed}, \mu_{*} \gamma} & 0<\gamma \leq P \\ m^{\mathrm{fixed}, \mu_{*}, \gamma-P} & P<\gamma \leq P+R\end{cases}
$$

$$
p_{y}^{\mathrm{dupli}, \gamma}= \begin{cases}p_{y}^{\mathrm{fixed}, 0} & \gamma=0 \\ \frac{1}{2} p_{y}^{\mathrm{fixed}, \gamma} & 0<\gamma \leq R \\ p_{y}^{\mathrm{fixed}, \gamma} & R<\gamma \leq P \\ \frac{1}{2} p_{y}^{\mathrm{fixed}, \gamma-P} & P<\gamma \leq P+R\end{cases}
$$

$$
\text{along with } \quad p_{\mathbf{h}}(\gamma)=
\begin{cases}
p_{\mathbf{h}}^{\text{given}}(0) & \gamma=0 \\
\frac{1}{2} p_{\mathbf{h}}^{\text{given}}(\gamma) & 0<\gamma \leq R \\
p_{\mathbf{h}}^{\text{given}}(\gamma) & R<\gamma \leq P \\
\frac{1}{2} p_{\mathbf{h}}^{\text{given}}(\gamma-P) & P<\gamma \leq P+R,
\end{cases}
$$

where the hidden units $\gamma \in \{P+1,..., P+R\}$ and their corresponding order parameters are duplicates, or copies, of $\gamma \in \{1,..., R\}$. $\gamma=0$ can also be duplicated, but the result is less interesting. By definition (see Eqs. 4.21), $\zeta_{y}^{\gamma}\left(\overline{\mathbf{p}}^{\text{dupli}} ; p_{\mathbf{h}}\right)=\lambda_{y}\left(\overline{\mathbf{p}}^{\text{dupli}} ; p_{\mathbf{h}}\right)+\omega^{\gamma}\left(\overline{\mathbf{p}}^{\text{dupli}} ; p_{\mathbf{h}}\right)$, where $\lambda_{y}\left(\overline{\mathbf{p}}^{\text{dupli}} ; p_{\mathbf{h}}\right)$ and $\omega^{\gamma}\left(\overline{\mathbf{p}}^{\text{dupli}} ; p_{\mathbf{h}}\right)$ are the $\lambda_{y}$ and $\omega^{\gamma}$ solving

$$
\begin{aligned}
\omega^{\gamma} & =\frac{1}{p_{\mathbf{h}}(\gamma)} \sum_{y=0}^{C} \frac{\omega^{\gamma}}{\lambda_{y}+\omega^{\gamma}} \bar{p}_{y}^{\text{dupli,}\gamma} \\
& =\frac{1}{p_{\mathbf{h}}^{\text{given}}(\gamma)} \sum_{y=0}^{C} \frac{\omega^{\gamma}}{\lambda_{y}+\omega^{\gamma}} \bar{p}_{y}^{\text{fixed,}\gamma}
\end{aligned}
$$

$$
\begin{aligned}
\lambda_{y} & =\frac{1}{p_{\mathbf{q}}(y)} \sum_{\gamma=0}^{P+R} \frac{\lambda_{y}}{\lambda_{y}+\omega^{\gamma}} \bar{p}_{y}^{\text{dupli,}\gamma} \\
& =\frac{1}{p_{\mathbf{q}}(y)} \sum_{\gamma=0}^{P} \frac{\lambda_{y}}{\lambda_{y}+\omega^{\gamma}} \bar{p}_{y}^{\text{fixed,}\gamma}.
\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore, $\zeta_{y}^{\gamma}\left(\overline{\mathbf{p}}^{\text{dupli}} ; p_{\mathbf{h}}\right)=\zeta_{y}^{\gamma}\left(\overline{\mathbf{p}}^{\text{fixed}} ; p_{\mathbf{h}}^{\text{given}}\right)$, and the saddle-point equations simplify to

$$
\begin{aligned}
\bar{x}_{i}^{\text{fixed,}\mu} & =\frac{1}{2}(1+\mathbb{I}(\mu>R)) \sum_{\mu_{*}=1}^{P^{*}} x_{i}^{* \mu_{*}} \sum_{y=0}^{C} q_{y}^{* \mu_{*}} \sigma_{\mu}\left(\beta_{\text{eff}} m^{\text{fixed,}\mu_{*}}+\log \left[\mathbf{p}_{y}^{\text{fixed}}\right]\right) \\
\bar{p}_{y}^{\text{fixed,}\gamma} & =\sum_{\mu_{*}=1}^{P^{*}} q_{y}^{* \mu_{*}} \sigma_{\gamma}\left(\beta_{\text{eff}} m^{\text{fixed,}\mu_{*}}+\log \left[\mathbf{p}_{y}^{\text{fixed}}\right]\right) \\
m^{\text{fixed,}\mu_{*} \mu} & =\varsigma\left(2 \beta_{\text{eff}} \varrho \sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left[\overline{\mathbf{x}}_{i}^{* \text{fixed,}\mu}\right]^{2}}\right) \frac{\sum_{i=1}^{N} x_{i}^{* \mu_{*}} \overline{\mathbf{x}}_{i}^{* \text{fixed,}\mu}}{\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left[\overline{\mathbf{x}}_{i}^{* \text{fixed,}\mu}\right]^{2}}} \\
p_{y}^{\text{fixed,}\gamma} & =\frac{\bar{p}_{y}^{\text{fixed,}\gamma}}{\zeta_{y}^{\gamma}\left(\overline{\mathbf{p}}^{\text{fixed}} ; p_{\mathbf{h}}^{\text{given}}\right)},
\end{aligned}
$$

where $\mathbb{I}(\mu>R)$ is the indicator function equal to 1 when $\mu>R$ and 0 otherwise. Assume that $\varrho \rightarrow \infty$ so that $\varsigma\left(2 \beta_{\text{eff}} \varrho \sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left[\bar{x}_{i}^{\mu}\right]^{2}}\right) \rightarrow \mathbb{I}\left(\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left[\bar{x}_{i}^{\mu}\right]^{2}}>0\right)$, then the saddle-point equations are the same no matter how the prefactor of $\frac{1}{2}(1+\mathbb{I}(\mu>R))$ affects the norm of $\bar{x}_{i}^{\mu}$, and Eqs. (4.13) are a fixed point of the saddle-point equations with $P+R$ hidden units. In particular, Eq. (4.13) is a stationary point of the loss (Eq. 4.4) when $\beta_{\text{eff}}=\beta$.

For the rest of this Appendix, all sums are understood as having the same bounds as Eqs. (4.28) and (4.10).


The stability of any fixed point of the form $\mathbf{x} = \mathbf{F}(\mathbf{x})$, such as those of Eqs. (4.10), can be evaluated using the Jacobian matrix $\mathbf{J}$ of $\mathbf{F}$. If all eigenvalues $\lambda$ of the Jacobian satisfy $|\lambda|<1$, then the fixed point is stable. Conversely, if the Jacobian has an eigenvalue $\lambda$ with $|\lambda|>1$, then the fixed point is unstable. In particular, if the quadratic form $\mathbf{v}^T \mathbf{J} \mathbf{v}$ is larger than 1 for some $\mathbf{v}$ with $\|\mathbf{v}\|=1$, then the fixed point is unstable. For the rest of this Appendix, we evaluate the stability of Eqs. (4.10) with duplicated order parameters. Keeping $\overline{\mathbf{p}}$ and $\mathbf{p}$ fixed, the Jacobian of the saddle-point equation for $\overline{\mathbf{x}}$ is

$$
\begin{aligned}
\partial_{\bar{x}_{k}^{\nu}} \bar{x}_{j}^{\mu}= & \sum_{\mu_{*}} x_{j}^{* \mu_{*}} \partial_{\bar{x}_{k}^{\nu}} m^{\mu_{*} \nu} \sum_{y} q_{y}^{* \mu_{*}} \partial_{m^{\mu_{*} \nu}} \sigma_{\mu}\left(\beta_{\mathrm{eff}} m^{\mu_{*}}+\log \left[\mathbf{p}_{y}\right]\right) \\
= & \beta_{\mathrm{eff}} \sum_{\mu_{*}} x_{j}^{* \mu_{*}} \partial_{\bar{x}_{k}^{\nu}} m^{\mu_{*} \nu} \sum_{y} q_{y}^{* \mu_{*}} \sigma_{\mu}\left(\beta_{\mathrm{eff}} m^{\mu_{*}}+\log \left[\mathbf{p}_{y}\right]\right)\left(\delta_{\mu \nu}-\sigma_{\nu}\left(\beta_{\mathrm{eff}} m^{\mu_{*}}+\log \left[\mathbf{p}_{y}\right]\right)\right) \\
= & \beta_{\mathrm{eff}} \sum_{\mu_{*}} x_{j}^{* \mu_{*}} \frac{1}{\sqrt{\sum_{i}\left[\bar{x}_{i}^{\nu}\right]^{2}}}\left(x_{k}^{* \mu_{*}}-\left[\sum_{i} x_{i}^{* \mu_{*}} \tilde{x}_{i}^{\nu}\right] \tilde{x}_{k}^{\nu}\right) \\
& \sum_{y} q_{y}^{* \mu_{*}} \sigma_{\mu}\left(\beta_{\mathrm{eff}} m^{\mu_{*}}+\log \left[\mathbf{p}_{y}\right]\right)\left(\delta_{\mu \nu}-\sigma_{\nu}\left(\beta_{\mathrm{eff}} m^{\mu_{*}}+\log \left[\mathbf{p}_{y}\right]\right)\right),
\end{aligned}
$$

where $\tilde{x}_{j}^{\nu}=\frac{\bar{x}_{j}^{\nu}}{\sqrt{\sum_{i}\left[\bar{x}_{i}^{\nu}\right]^{2}}}$. Suppose that $\bar{x}_{i}^{\mu}, \bar{p}_{y}^{\gamma}, m^{\mu_{*} \gamma}$ and $p_{y}^{\gamma}$ are the duplicated parameters of Eq. (4.13) with $0<\mu, \nu \leq R$ or $P<\mu, \nu \leq P+R$, then

$$
\begin{aligned}
\partial_{\bar{x}_{k}^{\nu}} \bar{x}_{j}^{\mu}= & \beta_{\mathrm{eff}} \sum_{\mu_{*}} x_{j}^{* \mu_{*}} \frac{1}{\sqrt{\sum_{i}\left[\bar{x}_{i}^{\nu}\right]^{2}}}\left(x_{k}^{* \mu_{*}}-\left[\sum_{i} x_{i}^{* \mu_{*}} \tilde{x}_{i}^{\nu}\right] \tilde{x}_{k}^{\nu}\right) \\
& \sum_{y} q_{y}^{* \mu_{*}} \frac{1}{2} \sigma_{\theta(\mu)}\left(\beta_{\mathrm{eff}} m^{\mathrm{fixed}, \mu_{*}}+\log \left[\mathbf{p}_{y}^{\mathrm{fixed}}\right]\right)\left(\delta_{\mu \nu}-\frac{1}{2} \sigma_{\theta(\nu)}\left(\beta_{\mathrm{eff}} m^{\mathrm{fixed}, \mu_{*}}+\log \left[\mathbf{p}_{y}^{\mathrm{fixed}}\right]\right)\right),
\end{aligned}
$$

$$
\text{where } \theta(\mu)=
\begin{cases}
\mu & 0<\mu \leq R \\
\mu-P & 0<\mu \leq P+R.
\end{cases}
$$

If $\beta_{\text {eff }}$ is relatively large (for instance of order $\sqrt{N}$ ), then the softmax $\sigma_{\theta(\mu)}$ splits the indices $\mu_{*}$ into a cover $\mathcal{S}$ of sets $\mathcal{S}(\mu)$ such that $\sigma_{\theta(\mu)}\left(\beta_{\text {eff }} m^{\text {fixed, } \mu_{*}}+\log \left[\mathbf{p}_{y}\right]\right) \approx \mathbb{I}\left(\mu_{*} \in \mathcal{S}(\mu)\right)$. Therefore, we have

$$
\begin{aligned}
\partial_{\bar{x}_{k}^{\nu}} \bar{x}_{j}^{\mu} \approx & \beta_{\mathrm{eff}} \sum_{\mu_{*}} x_{j}^{* \mu_{*}} \frac{1}{\sqrt{\sum_{i}\left[\bar{x}_{i}^{\nu}\right]^{2}}}\left(x_{k}^{* \mu_{*}}-\left[\sum_{i} x_{i}^{* \mu_{*}} \tilde{x}_{i}^{\nu}\right] \tilde{x}_{k}^{\nu}\right) \\
& \frac{1}{2} \mathbb{I}\left(\mu_{*} \in \mathcal{S}(\mu)\right)\left(\delta_{\mu \nu}-\frac{1}{2} \mathbb{I}\left(\mu_{*} \in \mathcal{S}(\nu)\right)\right).
\end{aligned}
$$

The subcovers $\mathcal{S}(0<\mu \leq P)$ and $\mathcal{S}(R<\mu \leq P+R)$ are both partitions of the indices $\mu_{*}$. As such, $\left[\partial_{\bar{x}_{k}^{\nu}} \bar{x}_{j}^{\mu}\right]_{\mu, \nu=1}^{P+R}$ is block diagonal with $2 \times 2$ blocks coupling the indices $0<\mu \leq R$ and $\nu=\mu+P$. Without

137

loss of generality, we investigate the stability of the block

$$
\begin{aligned}
& {\left[\begin{array}{cc}
\partial_{\bar{x}_{k}^{1}} \bar{x}_{j}^{1} & \partial_{\bar{x}_{k}^{P+1}} \bar{x}_{j}^{1} \\
\partial_{\bar{x}_{k}^{1}} \bar{x}_{j}^{P+1} & \partial_{\bar{x}_{k}^{P+1}} \bar{x}_{j}^{P+1}
\end{array}\right] } \\
& =\frac{1}{2} \frac{\beta_{\text {eff }}}{\sqrt{\sum_{i}\left[x_{i}^{\prime 1}\right]^{2}}} \sum_{\mu_{*} \in \mathcal{S}(1)}\left[\begin{array}{cc}
x_{j}^{* \mu_{*}}\left(x_{k}^{* \mu_{*}}-\left[\sum_{i} x_{i}^{* \mu_{*}} \tilde{x}_{i}^{1}\right] \tilde{x}_{k}^{1}\right) & -x_{j}^{* \mu_{*}}\left(x_{k}^{* \mu_{*}}-\left[\sum_{i} x_{i}^{* \mu_{*}} \tilde{x}_{i}^{1}\right] \tilde{x}_{k}^{1}\right) \\
-x_{j}^{* \mu_{*}}\left(x_{k}^{* \mu_{*}}-\left[\sum_{i} x_{i}^{* \mu_{*}} \tilde{x}_{i}^{1}\right] \tilde{x}_{k}^{1}\right) & x_{j}^{* \mu_{*}}\left(x_{k}^{* \mu_{*}}-\left[\sum_{i} x_{i}^{* \mu_{*}} \tilde{x}_{i}^{1}\right] \tilde{x}_{k}^{1}\right)
\end{array}\right].
\end{aligned}
$$

where $x_{i}^{\prime 1}=\sum_{\mu_{*} \in \mathcal{S}(1)} x_{i}^{* \mu_{*}}$. Let $\mathbf{u}^{1}$ be an arbitrary vector orthogonal to $\tilde{\mathbf{x}}^{1}$ and define

$$
v_{j}^{\mu}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}} \begin{cases}u_{j}^{1} & \mu=1 \\ -u_{j}^{1} & \mu=P+1,\end{cases}
$$

then we have the quadratic form

$$
\begin{gathered}
\sum_{\nu \in\{1, P+1\}} \sum_{k=1}^{N} v_{k}^{\nu} \partial_{\bar{x}_{k}^{\nu}} \bar{x}_{j}^{\mu}=\frac{\beta_{\text {eff }}}{\sqrt{\sum_{i}\left[x_{i}^{\prime 1}\right]^{2}}} \sum_{\mu_{*} \in \mathcal{S}(1)} \frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left[\begin{array}{c}
x_{j}^{* \mu_{*}} \sum_{k} x_{k}^{* \mu_{*}} u_{k}^{1} \\
-x_{j}^{* \mu_{*}} \sum_{k} x_{k}^{* \mu_{*}} u_{k}^{1}
\end{array}\right] \\
\sum_{\mu, \nu \in\{1, P+1\}} \sum_{j, k=1}^{N} v_{j}^{\mu} v_{k}^{\nu} \partial_{\bar{x}_{k}^{\nu}} \bar{x}_{j}^{\mu}=\frac{\beta_{\text {eff }}}{\sqrt{\sum_{i}\left[x_{i}^{\prime 1}\right]^{2}}} \sum_{\mu_{*} \in \mathcal{S}(1)}\left[\sum_{j} x_{j}^{* \mu_{*}} u_{j}^{1}\right]^{2}.
\end{gathered}
$$

If $\mathbf{u}^{1}$ is orthogonal to all the $\mathbf{x}^{* \mu_{*}}$ such that $\mu_{*} \in \mathcal{S}(1)$, and in particular if $\mathcal{S}(1)$ contains a single pattern $\mathbf{x}^{* \mu_{*}}=\tilde{\mathbf{x}}^{1}$, then $\sum_{\mu_{*} \in \mathcal{S}(1)}\left[\sum_{j} x_{j}^{* \mu_{*}} u_{j}^{1}\right]^{2}=0$, so the quadratic form vanishes. In this case, $\mathbf{u}^{1}$ is a stable direction of the saddle-point equations. Otherwise, the quadratic form does not vanish, so there is a $\beta_{\text {split }}$ such that the direction $\mathbf{u}^{1}$ is unstable when $\beta_{\text {eff }}>\beta_{\text {split }}$.

### 4.K Splitting steepest descent

Steps 5 and 11 of splitting steepest descent (Alg. 1) involve the splitting matrices $\mathcal{S}_\mu(\mathbf{w}, \mathbf{p})$ derived in [87]. In this appendix, we explain their role in the algorithm. Consult [87] for a detailed explanation of their interpretation and theoretical underpinnings.

Define the thresholds $\tau_{\text{thres}} \in (0,1]$ and $\lambda_{\text{thres}} \leq 0$. At step 5 of Alg. (1), we duplicate the hidden units corresponding to the $R \leq \min \{\tau_{\text{thres}} P_{\text{cur}}, P_{\text{max}} - P_{\text{cur}}\}$ most negative minimum eigenvalues $\lambda_{\text{min}}^\mu$ of the splitting matrices $\mathcal{S}_\mu(\mathbf{w}, \mathbf{p})$ such that $\lambda_{\text{min}}^\mu \leq \lambda_{\text{thres}}$ [87]. To make Fig. (4.7), we pick $\tau_{\text{thres}} = 1$ and $\lambda_{\text{thres}} \approx 0$. Our splitting matrices are

$$
\mathcal{S}_\mu(\mathbf{w}, \mathbf{p}) = -\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}^*, y^*} \left[ \frac{p_y^\mu \bar{\nabla}_{\mathbf{w}^\mu} \bar{\nabla}_{\mathbf{w}^\mu}^T \exp \left( \beta_{\text{eff}} \sum_{i=1}^N w_i^\mu x_i \right)}{\sum_{\nu=1}^P p_y^\nu \exp \left( \beta_{\text{eff}} \sum_{i=1}^N w_i^\nu x_i \right) + p_y^0 \frac{\Omega_N(\beta)}{\Omega_N(0)}} \right],
$$

where $\bar{\nabla}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}$ is the gradient constrained to the unit hypersphere $S^{N-1}$ (see Appendix 4.C) and $\beta_{\text{eff}}$ can be written explicitly in terms of $\beta$ as $\beta_{\text{eff}} = \varsigma \beta$ (see Eqs. 4.12 and 4.10). At step 11 of Alg. (1), we break permutation symmetries in the memories $\mathbf{w}^\mu$ by descending along the eigenvectors $\mathbf{u}^\mu \in S^{N-1}$ corresponding to the eigenvalues $\lambda_{\text{min}}^\mu$. To be more precise, we update the memories $\mathbf{w}^\mu$ and their duplicates $\mathbf{w}^{\text{dupli},\mu}$ according to $\mathbf{w}^\mu \leftarrow \mathbf{w}^\mu + \delta \mathbf{u}^\mu$ and $\mathbf{w}^{\text{dupli},\mu} \leftarrow \mathbf{w}^{\text{dupli},\mu} - \delta \mathbf{u}^\mu$, respectively, where $\delta$ is a relatively small learning rate [87]. In physics terminology, the eigenvectors $\mathbf{u}^\mu$ are excitation modes that break the permutation symmetries of the memories. $N$ and $P$ are generally large, so it is prohibitively expensive to store $\mathcal{S}_\mu(\mathbf{w}, \mathbf{p})$ explicitly for all hidden units $1 \leq \mu \leq P$. Therefore, as proposed in [88], we find the eigenvectors $\mathbf{u}^\mu$ and their eigenvalues $\lambda_{\text{min}}^\mu$ by minimizing the Rayleigh quotients $Q_\mu[\mathbf{w}, \mathbf{p}]: S^{N-1} \ni \mathbf{u}^\mu \mapsto [\mathbf{u}^\mu]^T \mathcal{S}_\mu(\mathbf{w}, \mathbf{p}) \mathbf{u}^\mu$, which can be evaluated without constructing $\mathcal{S}_\mu(\mathbf{w}, \mathbf{p})$ explicitly. To derive $Q_\mu[\mathbf{w}, \mathbf{p}]$, we first compute $\bar{\nabla}_{\mathbf{w}^\mu} \bar{\nabla}_{\mathbf{w}^\mu}^T \exp(\beta_{\text{eff}} \sum_i w_i^\mu x_i)$. Given $f(\boldsymbol{\theta}) = \sum_i \theta_i x_i$, we find

$$
\bar{\nabla}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}} \bar{\nabla}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^T \exp(\beta_{\text{eff}} f(\boldsymbol{\theta})) = \exp(\beta_{\text{eff}} f(\boldsymbol{\theta})) \left( \beta_{\text{eff}} \bar{\nabla}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}} \bar{\nabla}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^T f(\boldsymbol{\theta}) + \beta_{\text{eff}}^2 \bar{\nabla}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}} f(\boldsymbol{\theta}) \bar{\nabla}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^T f(\boldsymbol{\theta}) \right).
$$

As mentioned at the beginning of Appendix 4.C, $\bar{\nabla}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}} f(\boldsymbol{\theta})$ is the unrestricted gradient of $f(\boldsymbol{\theta})$ projected onto the tangent space of $\boldsymbol{\theta}$. It remains to calculate $\bar{\nabla}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}} \bar{\nabla}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^T f(\boldsymbol{\theta})$. Following [223], we find it to be the iterated projected gradient

$$
\begin{aligned}
\left[ \bar{\nabla}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}} \bar{\nabla}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^T f(\boldsymbol{\theta}) \right]_{i\ell} &= \sum_k (\delta_{k\ell} - \theta_k \theta_\ell) \partial_{\theta_i} \left( \sum_j (\delta_{jk} - \theta_j \theta_k) \partial_{\theta_j} \left[ \sum_h \theta_h x_h \right] \right) \\
&= \sum_k (\delta_{k\ell} - \theta_k \theta_\ell) \partial_{\theta_i} \left( x_k - \left[ \sum_j \theta_j x_j \right] \theta_k \right) \\
&= -\sum_k (\delta_{k\ell} - \theta_k \theta_\ell) \left( x_i \theta_k + \left[ \sum_j \theta_j x_j \right] \delta_{ik} \right) \\
&= -\left[ \sum_j \theta_j x_j \right] (\delta_{i\ell} - \theta_i \theta_\ell),
\end{aligned}
$$

143

so we obtain

$$
Q_{\mu}[\mathbf{w}, \mathbf{p}]\left(\mathbf{u}^{\mu}\right)=-\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}^{*}, y^{*}}\left[\frac{p_{y}^{\mu} \exp \left(\beta_{\text {eff }} \sum_{i=1}^{N} w_{i}^{\mu} x_{i}\right)}{\sum_{\nu=1}^{P} p_{y}^{\nu} \exp \left(\beta_{\text {eff }} \sum_{i=1}^{N} w_{i}^{\nu} x_{i}\right)+p_{y}^{0} \frac{\Omega_{N}(\beta)}{\Omega_{N}(0)}} F\left(\mathbf{u}^{\mu} ; \mathbf{w}^{\mu}, \mathbf{x}\right)\right]
$$

$$
\begin{aligned}
\text { where } \quad F(\boldsymbol{\varphi} ; \boldsymbol{\theta}, \mathbf{x}) & =\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{T}\left[\beta_{\mathrm{eff}} \bar{\nabla}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}} \bar{\nabla}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{T} f(\boldsymbol{\theta})+\beta_{\mathrm{eff}}^{2} \bar{\nabla}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}} f(\boldsymbol{\theta}) \bar{\nabla}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{T} f(\boldsymbol{\theta})\right] \boldsymbol{\varphi} \\
& =\beta_{\mathrm{eff}}^{2}\left(\sum_{k} \varphi_{k} x_{k}-\left[\sum_{k} \varphi_{k} \theta_{k}\right]\left[\sum_{j} \theta_{j} x_{j}\right]\right)^{2} \\
& +\beta_{\mathrm{eff}}\left[\sum_{j} \theta_{j} x_{j}\right]\left[\sum_{i} \varphi_{i} \theta_{i}-1\right]\left[\sum_{\ell} \varphi_{\ell} \theta_{\ell}+1\right].
\end{aligned}
$$

We can directly minimize $Q[\mathbf{w}, \mathbf{p}](\mathbf{u})=\sum_{\mu} Q_{\mu}[\mathbf{w}, \mathbf{p}]\left(\mathbf{u}^{\mu}\right)$ to find the set of all eigenvectors $\mathbf{u}^{\mu}$ simultaneously. However, it is more convenient to integrate the eigenvector calculations into the DAM architecture. As such, we define the modified loss

$$
\begin{aligned}
\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}(\mathbf{w}, \mathbf{p}, \mathbf{u}) & =-\log \left[\sum_{\mu=1}^{P} p_{y}^{\mu} \exp \left(\varsigma \beta \sum_{i=1}^{N} w_{i}^{\mu} x_{i}\right)\left[1+\epsilon F\left(\mathbf{u}^{\mu} ; \mathbf{w}^{\mu}, \mathbf{x}\right)\right]+p_{y}^{0} \frac{\Omega_{N}(\beta)}{\Omega_{N}(0)}\right] \\
& =-\log \left[\sum_{\mu=1}^{P} p_{y}^{\mu} \exp \left(\varsigma \beta \sum_{i=1}^{N} w_{i}^{\mu} x_{i}+\log \left[1+\epsilon F\left(\mathbf{u}^{\mu} ; \mathbf{w}^{\mu}, \mathbf{x}\right)\right]\right)+p_{y}^{0} \frac{\Omega_{N}(\beta)}{\Omega_{N}(0)}\right],
\end{aligned}
$$

so that the four gradients used to trained the DAM can be calculated using the equations

$$
\nabla_{\mathbf{u}} \mathcal{Q}[\mathbf{w}, \mathbf{p}](\mathbf{u})=\lim _{\epsilon \rightarrow 0}\left\{\frac{1}{\epsilon} \nabla_{\mathbf{u}} \mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}(\mathbf{w}, \mathbf{p}, \mathbf{u})\right\},
$$

$$
\nabla_{\mathbf{w}} \mathcal{L}(\mathbf{w}, \mathbf{p})=\nabla_{\mathbf{w}} \mathcal{L}_{0}(\mathbf{w}, \mathbf{p}, \mathbf{u})
$$

$$
\nabla_{\mathbf{p}} \mathcal{L}(\mathbf{w}, \mathbf{p})=\nabla_{\mathbf{p}} \mathcal{L}_{0}(\mathbf{w}, \mathbf{p}, \mathbf{u})
$$

$$
\text { and } \quad \nabla_{\beta} \mathcal{L}(\mathbf{w}, \mathbf{p})=\nabla_{\beta} \mathcal{L}_{0}(\mathbf{w}, \mathbf{p}, \mathbf{u}) \text {. }
$$

This technique is based on the automatic differentiation trick proposed in [88]. We numerically implement the limit in the first equation by setting $\epsilon=0$ during loss evaluation and $\epsilon=1$ during gradient computation. When we optimize $\mathcal{Q}[\mathbf{w}, \mathbf{p}](\mathbf{u})$, the minima of the different Rayleigh quotients $Q_{\mu}[\mathbf{w}, \mathbf{p}]\left(\mathbf{u}^{\mu}\right)$ can span multiple orders of magnitude, so we normalize the gradients $\nabla_{\mathbf{u}^{\mu}} Q_{\mu}[\mathbf{w}, \mathbf{p}]\left(\mathbf{u}^{\mu}\right)$ by a running average of their magnitudes to facilitate convergence. This is inspired by the use of RMSProp in [88]. Moreover, we constrain the eigenvectors $\mathbf{u}^{\mu}$ and the gradients $\nabla_{\mathbf{u}^{\mu}} Q_{\mu}[\mathbf{w}, \mathbf{p}]\left(\mathbf{u}^{\mu}\right)$ to the unit hypersphere as we do for $\mathbf{w}^{\mu}$ (see Appendix 4.C). To make Fig. (4.7), we train the eigenvectors for 1 epoch, during which we monitor $\min _{\mu}\left\{\frac{1}{2}\left[\mathbf{u}^{\mu}\right]^{T} \nabla_{\mathbf{u}^{\mu}} \mathcal{Q}[\mathbf{w}, \mathbf{p}]\left(\mathbf{u}^{\mu}\right)\right\}=\min _{\mu}\left\{\mathcal{Q}[\mathbf{w}, \mathbf{p}]\left(\mathbf{u}^{\mu}\right)\right\} \sim \min _{\mu}\left\{\lambda_{\min }^{\mu}\right\}$ as a metric. The Rayleigh quotients converge very quickly, and further training of the eigenvectors is not necessary.

144

# Conclusion

In this thesis, we study various kinds of dense associative memory (DAM) models and restricted Boltzmann machines (RBMs) in the teacher-student setting.

In Chapter 2, we study a DAM called dense Hopfield network (dense HN). On the Nishimori line, we show that the phase transition where student dense HNs with a single pattern become capable of learning a dataset with $M$ samples generated by a teacher dense HN with a single pattern coincides with the spin-glass transition of dense HNs with $M$ random patterns. Outside the Nishimori line, we study the resistance of dense HNs to noise and adversarial attacks. In particular, we derive a formula quantifying the adversarial robustness of dense HNs at zero temperature, and we clarify why the adversarial robustness of dense HNs depends on the learning regime, as observed in [36].

In Chapter 3, we study RBMs with any finite number of hidden units. When the patterns incident to the $P^*$ hidden units of the teacher RBM are uncorrelated, we show that a student RBM with $P$ hidden units learns the data in the same way as $P$ separate RBMs with one hidden unit each, thus validating a conjecture formulated in [39]. Moreover, we show that student RBMs with more hidden units than their teacher learn a representation of the data where exactly $P^*$ of their patterns align themselves with those of the teacher while the $P-P^*$ remaining ones freeze in spin-glass states. We then argue that such RBMs can be used as toy models to study the lottery ticket hypothesis [9]. When the teacher patterns are correlated, we show that the student can adopt different learning strategies depending on the hyperparameters of the teacher-student setting and the number of samples in the training dataset.

In Chapter 4, we study a kind of DAM that fits into the framework of RBMs and is capable of both supervised and unsupervised classification. Based on insights from our theoretical analysis of the teacher-student setting, we propose a novel regularized loss function that makes training significantly more stable. Moreover, we show that the fixed points of relatively small DAMs are saddle points of larger DAMs, and we leverage this saddle-point hierarchy to considerably accelerate training using the splitting steepest descent algorithm introduced in [87].

A natural continuation of this work would be to study adversarial attacks in DAMs with many hidden units, such as the model studied in Chapter 4 [47]. In particular, it would be interesting to see if the methods that we use to study adversarial attacks in Chapter 2 can be generalized and used for this purpose in combination with those used to study adversarial attacks in linear models [18, 19, 20].

One could also extend this work by studying DAM and RBM training dynamics with analytical calculations, the statistical mechanics approach of [31, 32] being a possible starting point. First, it would be interesting to build a theoretical model of the training dynamics of the lottery ticket experiment presented in Chapter 3 [46],

as it could provide crucial insight into the lottery ticket hypothesis. In particular, it could help us to understand if the initial conditions that make certain subnetworks of large NNs converge especially quickly have common properties that can be exploited to design novel initialization schemes leading to faster training. Second, it would also be interesting to study analytically the learning dynamics of the DAM studied in Chapter 4 [47], particularly near saddle points, for a rigorous comparison of the standard training time with that of splitting steepest descent.

To further our study of implicitly low-dimensional learning, it would be interesting to investigate models with low-rank weights, such as in [31, 32]. For example, one could study the teacher-student setting in which a student RBM (or DAM) with weights constrained to have a low rank learns data generated by a teacher whose weights are structured in a similar way for representing data lying on a low-dimensional manifold [16, 17]. This kind of study could be used to investigate the benefits of low-rank constraints in exploiting the structure of data to improve the speed and quality of learning.

Finally, the interpretable learning dynamics [31, 32, 34] of RBMs and DAMs make them promising surrogate models of biological systems. It was notably shown that DAM learning dynamics is similar to cellular differentiation [34], and it would be interesting to see if the splitting steepest descent algorithm used to train DAMs in Chapter 4 [47] can be related to cellular division in an analogous way.
